    *   **Practical Significance:** The findings that spoken-genre data improves performance is significant and reinforces a push to train with more relevant data. However, The effect size is mild, especially in prominence task. The dataset creation and annotation side might not be completely reusable.

    *   **Theoretical Significance:** The paper addresses the gap between LLM performance on canonical language tasks and real-world language use. By focusing on spontaneous speech phenomena, the study offers insights into whether LLMs can capture aspects of human language processing that are not readily apparent from traditional evaluation metrics.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing LLM evaluation metrics and motivates the need for a more ecologically valid approach.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The experimental design is relatively sound, with multiple models, datasets, and languages considered.
    *   **Interesting Results:** The results show that models trained on conversational data perform better on speech-related tasks, which supports the argument that the type of training data matters for capturing real-world language phenomena.
    *   **Multilingual perspective.** Extends beyond solely English benchmarks.
    *   **Addresses an important gap.** LLMs can produce text but do they represent the complexity of natural speech as humans do?

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation focuses on only two production variables (speech reductions and prosodic prominence). While these are important aspects of spontaneous speech, they do not fully capture the complexity of human language production, or the specificities of each languages.

    *   **Task Triviality:** As acknowledged in the paper, there's a risk that the task is too trivial, with models simply learning statistical associations between words and their likelihood of being reduced or prominent. The correlation analyses with perplexity provide some evidence that this is not the whole story, but further investigation is needed.

    *   **Modest Improvements:** The improvements in performance achieved by conversational models are not always substantial. This suggests that while incorporating spontaneous speech data is beneficial, it may not be a game-changer. More extensive investigation with stronger effects could have been done.

    *   **Data Set Challenges:** The lack of a perfectly matching multi-lingual setting for all of the model variables, combined with some non-ideal annotation tools on the "ground truth" data limits the conclusiveness.

    *   **No human evaluation**. A comparison with human judgements on the same dataset could validate their findings.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **6.5/10**.

*   **Rationale:**

    *   The paper addresses a relevant and important problem in the field of LLM evaluation.

    *   The core idea and methodology are moderately novel.

    *   The results are interesting and provide some support for the authors' argument.

    *   However, the limited scope of the evaluation, modest improvements in performance, and the task's potential triviality prevent a higher score.  More thorough cross-lingual comparisons and a dataset with improved annotation could considerably raise the score, together with a human-validated experiment. The paper provides a valuable starting point for future research, but the findings are not yet conclusive enough to be considered a major breakthrough. This score represents a balance between recognizing the paper's potential and acknowledging its limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### DriveMoE: Mixture-of-Experts for Vision-Language-Action Model in End-to-End Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16278v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenjie Yang, Yilin Chai, Xiaosong Jia, Qifeng Li, Yuqian Shao, Xuekai Zhu, Haisheng Su, Junchi Yan
- **Abstract**: End-to-end autonomous driving (E2E-AD) demands effective processing of multi-view sensory data and robust handling of diverse and complex driving scenarios, particularly rare maneuvers such as aggressive turns. Recent success of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture in Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrates that specialization of parameters enables strong scalability. In this work, we propose DriveMoE, a novel MoE-based E2E-AD framework, with a Scene-Specialized Vision MoE and a Skill-Specialized Action MoE. DriveMoE is built upon our $\pi_0$ Vision-Language-Action (VLA) baseline (originally from the embodied AI field), called Drive-$\pi_0$. Specifically, we add Vision MoE to Drive-$\pi_0$ by training a router to select relevant cameras according to the driving context dynamically. This design mirrors human driving cognition, where drivers selectively attend to crucial visual cues rather than exhaustively processing all visual information. In addition, we add Action MoE by training another router to activate specialized expert modules for different driving behaviors. Through explicit behavioral specialization, DriveMoE is able to handle diverse scenarios without suffering from modes averaging like existing models. In Bench2Drive closed-loop evaluation experiments, DriveMoE achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance, demonstrating the effectiveness of combining vision and action MoE in autonomous driving tasks. We will release our code and models of DriveMoE and Drive-$\pi_0$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "DriveMoE" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DriveMoE, a novel Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) based framework for end-to-end autonomous driving. It addresses limitations of existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models in handling multi-view sensory data and diverse driving scenarios. DriveMoE features a Scene-Specialized Vision MoE, which dynamically selects relevant camera views based on the driving context, and a Skill-Specialized Action MoE, which activates specialized expert modules for different driving behaviors. The framework is built upon the authors' extended VLA foundation model Drive-π.  Experiments on the Bench2Drive benchmark demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, highlighting the effectiveness of combining vision and action MoE in autonomous driving. The authors promise to release their code and models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the application of MoE principles, commonly used in Large Language Models (LLMs), to the vision and action domains within autonomous driving. The specific implementation of Scene-Specialized Vision MoE and Skill-Specialized Action MoE, while not entirely conceptually groundbreaking, demonstrates a practical application of existing MoE ideas to a specific AD problem. Dynamic camera selection is somewhat explored in prior works, but this is a novel implementation within a VLA and MoE framework. The Skill-Specialized Action MoE, leveraging a flow-matching planner and explicitly addressing rare maneuvers, represents a valuable engineering contribution.
* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing practical limitations in end-to-end autonomous driving, particularly in handling long-tail scenarios and the computational demands of multi-view processing. The performance gains on the Bench2Drive benchmark, including a considerable improvement in the driving score and success rate, demonstrate the potential of the approach. By reducing mode averaging, the model also addresses the problem of rare behaviors in autonomous driving.  Further, the potential to leverage LLMs-like scalability via the MoE architecture is promising.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** State-of-the-art results on a standard benchmark provide convincing evidence of the framework's effectiveness.
    * **Addresses Practical Limitations:** The paper directly tackles the inefficiencies and limitations of existing end-to-end driving models in handling complex scenarios and computational cost.
    * **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework and experimental setup.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of MoE with vision and action is novel for AD, the underlying concepts of MoE, dynamic camera selection, and hierarchical planning are not entirely new.
    * **Limited Generalization Evidence:** Bench2Drive is a simulated environment, and the real-world transferability of the approach remains to be demonstrated.
    * **Implementation Details:** While the paper provides details, a deeper dive into the specific architecture of the router modules and the training process would further strengthen the work.
    * **Missing Ablation Results:** More ablations comparing to other scene selection models.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in end-to-end autonomous driving by demonstrating the benefits of MoE-based architectures.  The release of code and models will further facilitate adoption and experimentation within the research community. However, the limited real-world validation could hamper a rapid uptake.

**Overall:** The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of end-to-end autonomous driving. It offers a practical and effective approach to address key limitations and opens up avenues for further research in leveraging MoE principles for autonomous systems. While the core concepts are not revolutionary, the specific implementation and experimental validation make it a valuable addition to the literature.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### HiMATE: A Hierarchical Multi-Agent Framework for Machine Translation Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16281v1)
- **Authors**: Shijie Zhang, Renhao Li, Songsheng Wang, Philipp Koehn, Min Yang, Derek F. Wong
- **Abstract**: The advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) enables flexible and interpretable automatic evaluations. In the field of machine translation evaluation, utilizing LLMs with translation error annotations based on Multidimensional Quality Metrics (MQM) yields more human-aligned judgments. However, current LLM-based evaluation methods still face challenges in accurately identifying error spans and assessing their severity. In this paper, we propose HiMATE, a Hierarchical Multi-Agent Framework for Machine Translation Evaluation. We argue that existing approaches inadequately exploit the fine-grained structural and semantic information within the MQM hierarchy. To address this, we develop a hierarchical multi-agent system grounded in the MQM error typology, enabling granular evaluation of subtype errors. Two key strategies are incorporated to further mitigate systemic hallucinations within the framework: the utilization of the model's self-reflection capability and the facilitation of agent discussion involving asymmetric information. Empirically, HiMATE outperforms competitive baselines across different datasets in conducting human-aligned evaluations. Further analyses underscore its significant advantage in error span detection and severity assessment, achieving an average F1-score improvement of 89% over the best-performing baseline. We make our code and data publicly available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/HiMATE-Anony.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HiMATE, a hierarchical multi-agent framework for machine translation evaluation (MTE). It addresses the limitations of existing LLM-based evaluation methods, which often struggle with accurately identifying error spans and assessing severity. HiMATE leverages the Multidimensional Quality Metrics (MQM) framework's hierarchical structure to create a multi-agent system where each agent specializes in evaluating specific error categories (tier-2 subtype errors) within broader categories (tier-1 errors).  The framework uses a three-stage approach: 1) subtype evaluation by individual agents, 2) self-reflection by the agents to revise judgments, and 3) collaborative discussion between agents from different tiers for uncertain cases.  The paper empirically demonstrates that HiMATE outperforms competitive baselines in human-aligned evaluations, error span detection, and severity assessment across different datasets.  The code and data are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific application of a hierarchical multi-agent system to the MTE problem, grounding the agent topology in the MQM error typology. While multi-agent systems and LLMs have been used in MTE before (e.g., M-MAD), HiMATE's novel hierarchical error framework and its use of multi-agent collaboration is what sets it apart.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance comes from the improvement in human alignment and accuracy in error detection and severity assessment, particularly in error span identification.  Improving MTE is crucial as machine translation becomes more prevalent, and a robust evaluation system enables further improvement in translation quality.  The detailed error identification offered by HiMATE facilitates more targeted improvements of LLM-based MTE models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current LLM-based MTE methods.
    *   **Well-defined framework:** HiMATE's architecture and three-stage process are well-explained.
    *   **Strong empirical results:**  HiMATE consistently outperforms baselines across various datasets and models. The ablation studies demonstrate the contribution of each stage.
    *   **Thorough analysis:** The error span detection analysis and domain-specific evaluations offer insights into HiMATE's strengths.
    *   **Public Availability:** The code and data are a plus for reproducibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Model Dependency:** While the paper shows that HiMATE performs well with different models it also indicates that the contribution of collaborative discission varies based on the instruction following skills of the backbone models, potentially limiting the overall benefits depending on the model used.
    *   **Limited Language Pairs:** The study primarily focuses on ZH-EN and EN-DE language pairs. While these are important, demonstrating effectiveness on more diverse and lower-resource language pairs would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Runtime performance:** There are no reports regarding runtime performance, and a multi-agent system such as this has the potential to be very computationally expensive
*   **Potential Influence:** HiMATE has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Providing a more accurate and interpretable MTE system.
    *   Guiding the development of more targeted improvements in machine translation.
    *   Inspiring further research into hierarchical multi-agent systems for other NLP tasks.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper presents a novel approach to machine translation evaluation with strong empirical results and thorough analysis. While there are limitations related to language pairs and dependence on backbone model characteristics, the improvements in human alignment, error detection, and interpretability demonstrate a significant contribution to the field. Given that the multi-agent system is very well structured and leverages the MQM hierarchy, the paper should spur future research into how similar methods could be developed for other language tasks.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ARPO:End-to-End Policy Optimization for GUI Agents with Experience Replay
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16282v1)
- **Authors**: Fanbin Lu, Zhisheng Zhong, Shu Liu, Chi-Wing Fu, Jiaya Jia
- **Abstract**: Training large language models (LLMs) as interactive agents for controlling graphical user interfaces (GUIs) presents a unique challenge to optimize long-horizon action sequences with multimodal feedback from complex environments. While recent works have advanced multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) for reasoning and tool-using capabilities in LLMs, their application to GUI-based agents remains relatively underexplored due to the difficulty of sparse rewards, delayed feedback, and high rollout costs. In this paper, we investigate end-to-end policy optimization for vision-language-based GUI agents with the aim of improving performance on complex, long-horizon computer tasks. We propose Agentic Replay Policy Optimization (ARPO), an end-to-end RL approach that augments Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with a replay buffer to reuse the successful experience across training iterations. To further stabilize the training process, we propose a task selection strategy that filters tasks based on baseline agent performance, allowing the agent to focus on learning from informative interactions. Additionally, we compare ARPO with offline preference optimization approaches, highlighting the advantages of policy-based methods in GUI environments. Experiments on the OSWorld benchmark demonstrate that ARPO achieves competitive results, establishing a new performance baseline for LLM-based GUI agents trained via reinforcement learning. Our findings underscore the effectiveness of reinforcement learning for training multi-turn, vision-language GUI agents capable of managing complex real-world UI interactions. Codes and models:https://github.com/dvlab-research/ARPO.git.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and significance score for the ARPO paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Agentic Replay Policy Optimization (ARPO), a reinforcement learning approach designed to train vision-language-based agents for controlling graphical user interfaces (GUIs).  ARPO enhances Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) by incorporating a replay buffer to reuse successful past experiences. This is particularly important due to the sparse and delayed rewards common in GUI environments. To further improve training, the paper proposes a task selection strategy that prioritizes tasks where a baseline agent demonstrates some initial success. The paper demonstrates that ARPO achieves competitive results on the OSWorld benchmark, establishing a new performance baseline for reinforcement learning-trained LLM-based GUI agents. Experiments highlight the effectiveness of ARPO, with the finding that it is particularly beneficial for in-domain tasks while providing moderate improvements to out-of-domain tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Practical Problem:** GUI automation with LLMs is a significant and actively researched area with numerous real-world applications.
    *   **Methodological Soundness:** The paper builds upon a strong existing framework (GRPO) and introduces modifications (replay buffer, task selection) that are well-motivated by the specific challenges of GUI environments (sparse rewards, high rollout costs).
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments on the OSWorld benchmark provide concrete evidence of ARPO's effectiveness. The ablation studies provide further insight into the contribution of each component (replay buffer, task selection).
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the ARPO approach and its benefits.
    *   **Self-Correction:** The qualitative example showing self-correction behavior is convincing and important.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of GRPO with experience replay and task selection is effective, the individual components are not entirely novel. Dynamic Sampling [28] already tackled the problem of training groups without reward variance and the principle of replaying the successful past is known. The paper builds on this idea with an experience replay.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** The finding that ARPO primarily improves in-domain performance, with only moderate gains in out-of-domain tasks, suggests that more work is needed to improve the agent's ability to generalize.
    *   **OSWorld as Benchmark:** While OSWorld is a valuable benchmark, its reliance on a fixed set of tasks may limit the assessment of true generalization capability. A demonstration of ARPO's effectiveness on novel tasks would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited Comparison to Existing RL Techniques for GUI Automation:** A more thorough comparison to other RL approaches specifically tailored for GUI interaction might be valuable. The related work section could be further strengthened.
    *   **Task Selection Bias:** It may cause a bias that is introduced by the task selection step, which makes the agent fail if given tasks which are not in its 128 learned tasks.

*   **Significance:**

    ARPO represents a solid step forward in training LLM-based GUI agents using reinforcement learning. By addressing the key challenges of sparse rewards and high rollout costs, the paper makes RL a more practical and effective approach for GUI automation. The techniques introduced in ARPO (replay buffer, task selection) are likely to be valuable for future research in this area. The study's insights into in-domain vs. out-of-domain performance also highlight important areas for future improvement in generalization capability.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper demonstrates a clear improvement over existing methods in a practical and important problem domain. The techniques are well-motivated, and the empirical validation is solid. The weaknesses lie in the incremental nature of the novelty and the limited generalization capability. The task selection step may introduce bias to the agent. While the paper offers a valuable contribution, it does not represent a groundbreaking advance that fundamentally changes the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Only Large Weights (And Not Skip Connections) Can Prevent the Perils of Rank Collapse
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16284v1)
- **Authors**: Josh Alman, Zhao Song
- **Abstract**: Attention mechanisms lie at the heart of modern large language models (LLMs). Straightforward algorithms for forward and backward (gradient) computation take quadratic time, and a line of work initiated by [Alman and Song NeurIPS 2023] and [Alman and Song NeurIPS 2024] has shown that quadratic time is necessary unless the model weights are small, in which case almost linear time algorithms are possible. In this paper, we show that large weights are necessary to avoid a strong preclusion to representational strength we call layer collapse, which means that the entire network can be approximated well by a network with only a single layer. Thus, the quadratic running time of attention is unavoidable for expressive transformers. The notion of layer collapse that we introduce is a variant on the notion of rank collapse from the work of [Dong, Cordonnier, and Loukas ICML 2021]. They showed that in Self Attention Networks with small weights and with skip connections, rank collapse must occur. This is typically interpreted as justifying the necessity of skip connections in expressive networks. However, our result shows that even with skip connections, if the weights are small, then layer collapse still occurs. Thus, only large weights, and not skip connections, can prevent these representational weaknesses.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the OCR output.

**Summary:**

The paper "Only Large Weights (And Not Skip Connections) Can Prevent the Perils of Rank Collapse" explores the representational limitations of attention mechanisms in large language models (LLMs). It focuses on the phenomenon of "layer collapse," which the authors define as a state where a multi-layer network can be well approximated by a single-layer network. The central claim is that large weight magnitudes (and not skip connections alone) are essential to prevent layer collapse and maintain the expressive power of transformers. The authors build upon prior work by Dong et al. (2021) who studied rank collapse. Their result challenges the common interpretation that skip connections are crucial for avoiding representational bottlenecks. By showing that layer collapse can still occur even *with* skip connections if the weights are small, the paper argues that large weights are a fundamental requirement for expressive transformers. The core argument involves proving that attention mechanisms in lower layers tend to exhibit rank collapse with small weights, enabling their removal without significant impact on the overall network output.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a novel and interesting perspective on the importance of weight magnitudes in transformer architectures. Prior work has largely focused on skip connections as a key factor in representational capacity. The authors provide a counter-argument. The specific definition of "layer collapse" is introduced here and serves as a useful concept to analyze this area.
*   **Significance:** The finding is significant as it questions a well-established belief that skip connections are the primary mechanism for avoiding representational collapse. If the claim holds, it suggests that future architectural designs and training strategies for transformers should prioritize maintaining substantial weight magnitudes. The paper also connects computational efficiency (small weights enable faster approximations) with expressivity (large weights are needed to prevent layer collapse) in an interesting way. This connection is also valuable.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of "layer collapse."
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The arguments are based on solid theoretical analysis and builds on previous research (DCL21), extending their work by including skip connections. The proofs, at least according to the description and sketched arguments, seem reasonably well-structured.
    *   **Counterintuitive Result:** The paper's main finding is somewhat unexpected and challenges prevailing assumptions, which makes it likely to generate discussion and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Informal Theorem Statement:** The main result (Theorem 1.2) is presented informally. The formal version in the appendix is not accessible, limiting a deep assessment of the result's rigor and scope.
    *   **Proof Sketch Only:** The paper only provides a proof sketch, deferring the detailed proofs to the appendix. This is a significant limitation since the soundness of the theoretical claims hinges on the detailed proofs.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** While the paper provides a theoretical argument, it lacks empirical validation. It's unclear how well these theoretical findings translate to real-world LLM training and performance. Do the effects of "layer collapse" predicted by the theory actually manifest in practice?
    *   **Practical Implications:** While the paper discusses the importance of large weight magnitudes, it is not completely clear what this translates to in a practical sense in the context of training methodologies. It would be helpful to see how their theoretical observations might lead to novel training strategies or architecture choices.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper could influence research on:

    *   **Transformer Architecture Design:** Researchers might consider alternative strategies that focus more on weight magnitude scaling.
    *   **Training Techniques:** New regularization or scaling techniques might emerge to maintain larger weight values during training.
    *   **Theoretical Understanding:** The paper could stimulate further theoretical investigation of representational limitations in deep learning models.

*   **Justification for Score:**

Given the combination of novelty, potential significance, but also the limitations of the proof sketches and lack of empirical validation, the paper's score is **6**. The interesting idea and challenge to established thinking are valuable, but a stronger empirical component and a more rigorous presentation of the proofs are needed to increase the score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Augmenting LLM Reasoning with Dynamic Notes Writing for Complex QA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16293v1)
- **Authors**: Rishabh Maheshwary, Masoud Hashemi, Khyati Mahajan, Shiva Krishna Reddy Malay, Sai Rajeswar, Sathwik Tejaswi Madhusudhan, Spandana Gella, Vikas Yadav
- **Abstract**: Iterative RAG for multi-hop question answering faces challenges with lengthy contexts and the buildup of irrelevant information. This hinders a model's capacity to process and reason over retrieved content and limits performance. While recent methods focus on compressing retrieved information, they are either restricted to single-round RAG, require finetuning or lack scalability in iterative RAG. To address these challenges, we propose Notes Writing, a method that generates concise and relevant notes from retrieved documents at each step, thereby reducing noise and retaining only essential information. This indirectly increases the effective context length of Large Language Models (LLMs), enabling them to reason and plan more effectively while processing larger volumes of input text. Notes Writing is framework agnostic and can be integrated with different iterative RAG methods. We demonstrate its effectiveness with three iterative RAG methods, across two models and four evaluation datasets. Notes writing yields an average improvement of 15.6 percentage points overall, with minimal increase in output tokens.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Augmenting LLM Reasoning with Dynamic Notes Writing for Complex QA":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "NotesWriting," a novel method to enhance iterative Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) for multi-hop question answering. Recognizing the limitations of standard iterative RAG due to lengthy contexts, information overload, and noise accumulation, NotesWriting generates concise, query-relevant notes from retrieved documents at each step. This reduces context overload, increases effective context length, improves LLM planning and reasoning, and can be integrated with any iterative RAG framework. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of NotesWriting with three baseline iterative RAG methods (IRCoT, FLARE, ReAct) and two LLMs (GPT-4o-mini, Llama 3.1-70B) across four multi-hop QA datasets. The results demonstrate significant improvements in performance (average of 15.6 percentage points) with minimal increase in output tokens.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dynamic note-taking within an iterative RAG framework exhibits moderate novelty. While summarization and compression techniques within RAG are not new, applying them *dynamically* and *query-relevant* at *each* iteration, specifically designed to mitigate the problems of iterative RAG, is a distinct contribution. The method's framework-agnostic nature also adds to its novelty, as it's not tied to a specific RAG architecture. However, it's important to acknowledge that recent works explore summarizing retrieved content within single-round or multi-round RAG. The novelty lies in the *combination* of these aspects in a *scalable* way.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial and well-recognized challenge in iterative RAG - managing context and dealing with information overload. The demonstrated performance improvements across multiple datasets and models show the practical value of NotesWriting. It has significant potential to make iterative RAG more effective and scalable for complex QA tasks, which are becoming increasingly important. The simplicity of the approach makes it appealing for adoption. The positive impact on both F1 score and GPT-4 as judge demonstrates increased overall quality and relevance of information generated. However, its societal impact also needs to be considered from the aspects of requested harmful context and factual incorrect retrieval (see additional points below).

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing iterative RAG methods.
    *   **Simple and Elegant Solution:** NotesWriting is a relatively straightforward and easy-to-implement method.
    *   **Framework Agnostic:** The plug-and-play design allows for easy integration with various RAG frameworks.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct extensive experiments with multiple models, datasets, and metrics.
    *   **Demonstrated Performance Gains:** Significant improvements are shown, solidifying the value of the paper.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** It shows how NotesWriting improves reasoning quality through reducing redundant queries.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Wikipedia API:** The reliance on Wikipedia API for fetching documents could limit generalizability and fairness, especially if access to Wikipedia changes. Also, the use of the 5 retrieved pages is limited, because it only focuses on single document (and is only limited to Wikipedia).
    *   **Potential for Hallucinations:** The note-taking LM could introduce hallucinations or inaccuracies, even if the notes are concise and query-relevant. This aspect requires more in-depth investigation, even though some results demonstrate better recovery.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to More Advanced Compression Techniques:** While the paper addresses simpler compression techniques, it would strengthen the paper by comparing against more sophisticated methods like neural compression, especially as it uses an additional LLM.
    *   **Impact of note size:** It would be helpful to do a sensitivity analysis to show the relationship between note size and model performance
    *   **Generalisability:** This approach needs to be checked on multiple non-Wikipedia QA datasets or closed domain datasets.
    *   **Societal impact:** It needs to be checked to the context of potentially requesting toxic or harmful information, even if moderated for downstream LLM usage.
    *   **Factual correctness:** It needs to be checked for factual accuracy of retrievals especially for multi-source retrieved sources.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and scalable iterative RAG systems.  The concept of dynamic note-taking could be adopted in other information retrieval and question answering scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The paper presents a practical and demonstrably effective solution to a significant problem in iterative RAG. While the novelty is incremental and the evaluation is not exhaustive, the demonstrated performance gains and framework-agnostic design make it a valuable contribution. The potential influence on the field is significant, making it worthy of attention from researchers and practitioners working on complex QA systems. However, potential hallucination and reliance on the Wikipedia API limits the overall benefits.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ToDi: Token-wise Distillation via Fine-Grained Divergence Control
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16297v1)
- **Authors**: Seongryong Jung, Suwan Yoon, DongGeon Kim, Hwanhee Lee
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) offer impressive performance but are impractical for resource-constrained deployment due to high latency and energy consumption. Knowledge distillation (KD) addresses this by transferring knowledge from a large teacher to a smaller student model. However, conventional KD, notably approaches like Forward KL (FKL) and Reverse KL (RKL), apply uniform divergence loss across the entire vocabulary, neglecting token-level prediction discrepancies. By investigating these representative divergences via gradient analysis, we reveal that FKL boosts underestimated tokens, while RKL suppresses overestimated ones, showing their complementary roles. Based on this observation, we propose Token-wise Distillation (ToDi), a novel method that adaptively combines FKL and RKL per token using a sigmoid-based weighting function derived from the teacher-student probability log-ratio. ToDi dynamically emphasizes the appropriate divergence for each token, enabling precise distribution alignment. We demonstrate that ToDi consistently outperforms recent distillation baselines using uniform or less granular strategies across instruction-following benchmarks. Extensive ablation studies and efficiency analysis further validate ToDi's effectiveness and practicality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ToDi: Token-wise Distillation via Fine-Grained Divergence Control":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently deploying large language models (LLMs) in resource-constrained environments through knowledge distillation (KD).  It identifies a limitation in conventional KD methods, specifically those utilizing Forward KL (FKL) and Reverse KL (RKL) divergences. The authors argue that applying these divergences uniformly across the vocabulary neglects token-level prediction discrepancies. Through gradient analysis, they demonstrate that FKL effectively boosts underestimated tokens, while RKL suppresses overestimated ones. To address this, the paper proposes a novel method called Token-wise Distillation (ToDi), which adaptively combines FKL and RKL per token using a sigmoid-based weighting function derived from the teacher-student probability log-ratio. ToDi dynamically emphasizes the appropriate divergence for each token, enabling precise distribution alignment. Experimental results on instruction-following benchmarks demonstrate ToDi's superiority over existing distillation baselines.  The paper also includes ablation studies and an efficiency analysis to further validate ToDi's effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the token-wise adaptive combination of FKL and RKL, a more granular approach than existing methods which often use uniform or vocabulary-level adaptations.  The use of the teacher-student probability log-ratio to derive the weighting function is a reasonable and grounded approach. The method avoids the common pitfall of uniformly applying knowledge across the entire vocabulary. The introduction of a generalized ToDi with varying scaling factors on FKL and RKL also adds to the methods novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the efficiency of deploying LLMs. By enabling more precise knowledge transfer from larger teacher models to smaller student models, ToDi contributes to reducing inference latency and energy consumption. The empirical results, showing consistent outperformance on instruction-following tasks, strongly suggest the practical utility of the method. The approach offers a principled way to address a core problem: how to effectively transfer knowledge from LLMs to smaller models for deployment in constrained environments. The ablations provide some evidence that per-token adjustments, instead of vocabulary level or fixed adjustments, are key.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-motivated, clearly articulating the limitations of existing KD techniques.
    *   The gradient analysis provides a solid theoretical foundation for the proposed method.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, covering multiple instruction-following benchmarks and teacher-student configurations.
    *   The ablation studies validate the importance of token-wise divergence control.
    *   The efficiency analysis demonstrates ToDi's practicality.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper assumes an identical vocabulary between teacher and student models, which is a limitation in some scenarios. This severely limits the practicality of the approach.
    *   The method requires access to the full token probability distribution of the teacher model, restricting its use to open-source LLMs that expose per-token logits. This is a major constraint that limits the applicability of the approach.
    *   While the experiments show improvements on the chosen benchmarks, the reliance on ROUGE-L as the primary metric is slightly limiting, especially given its known weaknesses in evaluating open-ended text generation. More recent automated or even human evaluations could strengthen the impact.
    *   The paper's introduction only lightly touches on potential drawbacks (memory usage or computational costs) compared to other approaches.
    *   While thorough, it relies on the commonly-used Dolly dataset. It would be more impactful if they tried it on more complicated instruction-following datasets or tasks, with more stringent performance requirements.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in knowledge distillation, particularly in the development of more fine-grained adaptation strategies. The core ideas behind ToDi could inspire new methods that combine different regularization techniques or utilize other sources of token-level information to guide knowledge transfer. The framework with scaling factors on FKL and RKL may also be useful for other areas of instruction following.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-justified approach to knowledge distillation with strong empirical evidence of its effectiveness. The theoretical motivation is clear, and the method addresses a relevant problem in the field of LLMs. However, the requirement for identical vocabularies and access to the teacher's token probabilities significantly limits its applicability and potential for widespread adoption, which detracts from the overall impact. Although the method has clear novelty and solid justification, its impact is limited by reliance on smaller benchmarks, and an extreme set of constraints. Nevertheless, the concept of token-wise divergence control is a valuable contribution that is likely to inspire future work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Flow Matching based Sequential Recommender Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16298v1)
- **Authors**: Feng Liu, Lixin Zou, Xiangyu Zhao, Min Tang, Liming Dong, Dan Luo, Xiangyang Luo, Chenliang Li
- **Abstract**: Generative models, particularly diffusion model, have emerged as powerful tools for sequential recommendation. However, accurately modeling user preferences remains challenging due to the noise perturbations inherent in the forward and reverse processes of diffusion-based methods. Towards this end, this study introduces FMRec, a Flow Matching based model that employs a straight flow trajectory and a modified loss tailored for the recommendation task. Additionally, from the diffusion-model perspective, we integrate a reconstruction loss to improve robustness against noise perturbations, thereby retaining user preferences during the forward process. In the reverse process, we employ a deterministic reverse sampler, specifically an ODE-based updating function, to eliminate unnecessary randomness, thereby ensuring that the generated recommendations closely align with user needs. Extensive evaluations on four benchmark datasets reveal that FMRec achieves an average improvement of 6.53% over state-of-the-art methods. The replication code is available at https://github.com/FengLiu-1/FMRec.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FMREC, a Flow Matching based Sequential Recommender Model designed to overcome limitations in diffusion-based recommendation systems.  Existing methods suffer from inaccurate user preference modeling due to noise in the forward process and randomness in the reverse process. FMREC addresses these issues by employing a straight flow trajectory and a modified loss tailored for recommendation. It incorporates a reconstruction loss to improve robustness against noise and uses a deterministic ODE-based reverse sampler to eliminate unnecessary randomness during recommendation generation. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of FMREC on four benchmark datasets, showing improved performance compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in adapting Flow Matching (a simplified diffusion model variant) for sequential recommendation and in the specific design choices made to address the shortcomings of existing diffusion-based recommenders.  Using a straight flow trajectory is a reasonable choice given the potential for error accumulation with curved trajectories, and the paper provides some theoretical justification for it. The introduction of a reconstruction loss to retain user preferences during the forward process is a practical and potentially impactful addition. The deterministic reverse sampler is a good fit given the emphasis on accurate, rather than diverse, recommendations.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to improve the accuracy of sequential recommendation by addressing noise issues inherent in diffusion models. If the reported performance gains hold up under scrutiny, this work could lead to more effective and personalized recommendation systems. The use of Flow Matching offers a potentially more efficient and stable alternative to traditional diffusion models. This is increasingly important in a field where model complexity often comes at the cost of interpretability. However, generative models applied to sequential recommendation have been a very recent and active field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with clear design choices.
    *   Empirical validation on multiple benchmark datasets.
    *   Ablation studies to justify design decisions (e.g., modified loss).
    *   Theoretical grounding for the benefits of straight trajectories (although limited).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical justification for straight trajectories is somewhat cursory. A more rigorous analysis of the error terms and their impact on the recommendation process would strengthen the paper.
    *   While the empirical results are promising, more detail regarding hyperparameter tuning and sensitivity analysis would be beneficial.
    *   The benefits of the model might be dataset specific - more discussion around what type of data it performs best on would be helpful.
    *   Generalization: Diffusion models are often very complex. Does FMREC improve generalization to unseen items or users?

*   **Impact:** FMREC has the potential to influence the direction of sequential recommendation research, particularly in the adoption of more efficient diffusion model variants like Flow Matching. Its focus on addressing noise and improving accuracy aligns with the practical needs of recommender systems. If the performance holds, it could be impactful. The release of code and datasets is useful for other researchers.

*   **Score Rationale:** While FMREC offers a novel approach and shows promising empirical results, the limitations described above prevent a higher score. The theoretical grounding could be more robust, and more discussion on hyperparameter sensitivity and dataset dependence would strengthen the paper. Furthermore, the field itself is very active, and this paper represents a well-done incremental improvement, rather than a transformative shift in the field.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### PMPO: Probabilistic Metric Prompt Optimization for Small and Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16307v1)
- **Authors**: Chenzhuo Zhao, Ziqian Liu, Xingda Wang, Junting Lu, Chaoyi Ruan
- **Abstract**: Prompt optimization offers a practical and broadly applicable alternative to fine-tuning for improving large language model (LLM) performance. However, existing methods often rely on costly output generation, self-critiquing abilities, or human-annotated preferences, which limit their scalability, especially for smaller or non-instruction-tuned models. We introduce PMPO (Probabilistic Metric Prompt Optimization), a unified framework that refines prompts using token-level cross-entropy loss as a direct, lightweight evaluation signal. PMPO identifies low-quality prompt segments by masking and measuring their impact on loss, then rewrites and selects improved variants by minimizing loss over positive and negative examples. Unlike prior methods, it requires no output sampling or human evaluation during optimization, relying only on forward passes and log-likelihoods. PMPO supports both supervised and preference-based tasks through a closely aligned loss-based evaluation strategy. Experiments show that PMPO consistently outperforms prior methods across model sizes and tasks: it achieves the highest average accuracy on BBH, performs strongly on GSM8K and AQUA-RAT, and improves AlpacaEval 2.0 win rates by over 19 points. These results highlight PMPO's effectiveness, efficiency, and broad applicability.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the PMPO paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Probabilistic Metric Prompt Optimization (PMPO), a novel framework for refining prompts in both small and large language models (LLMs).  Unlike existing methods that rely on costly output generation, self-critiquing, or human preferences, PMPO uses token-level cross-entropy loss as a direct and lightweight evaluation signal.  The framework identifies low-quality prompt segments by masking and measuring their impact on loss, and then rewrites these segments by minimizing loss over positive and negative examples. The method demonstrates strong performance across diverse tasks (BBH, GSM8K, AQUA-RAT, AlpacaEval 2.0) and varying model sizes, highlighting its effectiveness, efficiency, and broad applicability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** PMPO presents a significant departure from previous prompt optimization techniques by focusing on intrinsic loss-based evaluation, thereby circumventing the need for output sampling or human annotation. This approach is particularly novel in its applicability to smaller models where output generation is often unreliable. The unified framework for both supervised and preference-based tasks is also a notable contribution. While mask-based analysis isn't entirely new, its application and fine-tuning within this specific prompt optimization loop demonstrate originality.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in LLM development: the need for efficient and scalable prompt optimization. By avoiding output generation and external evaluation, PMPO offers a pathway to improved performance, particularly for resource-constrained scenarios.  The demonstration of consistent outperformance across diverse tasks and models underscores the method's broad applicability. The improvement in AlpacaEval 2.0 win rates is especially significant, as it addresses alignment issues without explicit preference annotations. Furthermore, PMPO's ability to function effectively with smaller models opens doors to broader deployment in scenarios where computational resources are limited. The exploration of cross-model transferability, though with some limitations on proprietary models, provides valuable insights into the nature of optimized prompts and their reliance on model-specific mechanisms.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Efficiency:** Loss-based evaluation significantly reduces computational cost.
    *   **Broad Applicability:** The framework works for both supervised and preference-based tasks.
    *   **Support for Small Models:** It's usable on models lacking introspection or multi-step reasoning.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** PMPO consistently outperforms existing methods.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Study:** Demonstrates the importance of various components.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Applicability to Proprietary Models:**  Reliance on token-level likelihoods restricts direct application to commercial APIs without those features.
    *   **Potential for Overfitting:** In extremely low-resource settings (e.g., using only one training example), the model may overfit to limited instances, reducing generalization.
    *   **Prompt Stability:** PMPO does not guarantee improvement in every single iteration.

*   **Potential Influence:** PMPO's loss-based evaluation approach has the potential to shift the paradigm of prompt optimization. It provides a more scalable and efficient method, particularly valuable for smaller models and alignment tasks.  The work will likely spur further research into intrinsic evaluation signals and model-adaptive optimization strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** PMPO presents a novel and significant contribution to prompt optimization, particularly in its ability to scale to smaller models and its avoidance of expensive output generation. The paper provides strong empirical evidence supporting its claims and a thorough ablation study. The main limitation is the restriction to models with token-level likelihood access, which hinders its direct application to proprietary systems. Despite this limitation, the work offers a substantial advancement and has the potential to influence future research directions in prompt engineering and LLM alignment.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Paired and Unpaired Image to Image Translation using Generative Adversarial Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16310v1)
- **Authors**: Gaurav Kumar, Soham Satyadharma, Harpreet Singh
- **Abstract**: Image to image translation is an active area of research in the field of computer vision, enabling the generation of new images with different styles, textures, or resolutions while preserving their characteristic properties. Recent architectures leverage Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) to transform input images from one domain to another. In this work, we focus on the study of both paired and unpaired image translation across multiple image domains. For the paired task, we used a conditional GAN model, and for the unpaired task, we trained it using cycle consistency loss. We experimented with different types of loss functions, multiple Patch-GAN sizes, and model architectures. New quantitative metrics - precision, recall, and FID score - were used for analysis. In addition, a qualitative study of the results of different experiments was conducted.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores paired and unpaired image-to-image translation using Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs). The authors implement and compare different loss functions (L1, L2, and combinations), PatchGAN sizes, and model architectures within the Pix2pix (paired) and CycleGAN (unpaired) frameworks.  They use multiple datasets (CMP facades, maps, cityscapes, horse to zebra) and evaluate the results both quantitatively (using precision, recall, and FID score) and qualitatively. The main contributions are the integration of training processes for both paired and unpaired translation, a study of various loss metrics and PatchGAN sizes, and the evaluation of the results with multiple quantitative metrics and a qualitative analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a systematic comparison of several important aspects of image-to-image translation, including different loss functions and PatchGAN sizes, which is useful for understanding their impact on performance.
    *   The use of multiple datasets increases the generalizability of the findings, although some datasets may be more representative than others.
    *   The inclusion of both quantitative metrics (precision, recall, FID) and qualitative analysis provides a comprehensive evaluation of the results. The addition of precision and recall, which aren't standard for GAN evaluation, is a good attempt to have more human-understandable metrics.
    *   The loss curves shown for the maps dataset provide insight into the training dynamics of the unpaired task.
    *   The investigation of skipping connections within the U-Net architecture is a standard, but sensible, experiment.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The work is largely an empirical comparison of existing methods. The paper doesn't introduce any novel architectures, loss functions, or training techniques. It implements existing methods (Pix2pix and CycleGAN) with minor variations.
    *   The novelty of simply using precision and recall isn't significant. While attempting to improve GAN evaluation metrics is valuable, the implementation here isn't particularly sophisticated, and doesn't convincingly address limitations of current techniques.
    *   The qualitative results, while included, are not thoroughly discussed, limiting the insight gained from them.
    *   The analysis of the effects of various hyperparameters can be expanded. For instance, the effect of lambda across many datasets.
    *   The paper could benefit from more in-depth analysis of the interplay between the different loss functions, PatchGAN sizes, and datasets.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper's novelty is relatively low. It doesn't propose new methods but rather evaluates existing methods with different configurations. Its significance is moderate. It serves as a useful benchmark and provides practical insights for researchers and practitioners working with image-to-image translation, however its findings aren't particularly paradigm-shifting. The experimentation serves mostly to reinforce common knowledge in the field.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's influence is likely to be limited due to the lack of novel contributions. It might be useful as a reference for practitioners seeking to optimize existing image-to-image translation pipelines, but it's unlikely to significantly advance the state of the art.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a decent empirical evaluation of established techniques. However, it lacks innovative contributions. Most results validate existing understanding. The main merit of the paper lies in combining all of the tested parameters in the GAN framework. Therefore, it is not a groundbreaking innovation, which is worth a high score.

**Score: 5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### EquivPruner: Boosting Efficiency and Quality in LLM-Based Search via Action Pruning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16312v1)
- **Authors**: Jiawei Liu, Qisi Chen, Jianshu Zhang, Quan Liu, Defu Lian
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at complex reasoning through search algorithms, yet current strategies often suffer from massive token consumption due to redundant exploration of semantically equivalent steps. Existing semantic similarity methods struggle to accurately identify such equivalence in domain-specific contexts like mathematical reasoning. To address this, we propose EquivPruner, a simple yet effective approach that identifies and prunes semantically equivalent actions during LLM reasoning search. We also introduce MathEquiv, the first dataset we created for mathematical statement equivalence, which enables the training of a lightweight equivalence detector. Extensive experiments across various models and tasks demonstrate that EquivPruner significantly reduces token consumption, improving searching efficiency and often bolstering reasoning accuracy. For instance, when applied to Qwen2.5-Math-7B-Instruct on GSM8K, EquivPruner reduced token consumption by 48.1\% while also improving accuracy. Our code is available at https://github.com/Lolo1222/EquivPruner.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary of the paper, followed by a rigorous and critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, culminating in a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper "EquivPruner: Boosting Efficiency and Quality in LLM-Based Search via Action Pruning" addresses the problem of redundancy in LLM-based search algorithms, particularly in domains like mathematical reasoning. The authors argue that current search strategies waste resources exploring semantically equivalent reasoning steps. To combat this, they propose *EquivPruner*, a method to identify and prune such equivalent actions during the LLM reasoning search process. They create *MathEquiv*, a new dataset for mathematical statement equivalence, to train a lightweight equivalence detector. Experiments demonstrate that EquivPruner reduces token consumption and improves, or maintains, accuracy across different models and tasks, showcasing enhanced search efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of pruning semantically equivalent actions in LLM search is *moderately* novel. While semantic redundancy has been observed in LLM outputs, this paper takes a direct approach to *pruning* these redundancies *during the search process* rather than post-processing or reranking. Prior work in semantic similarity or STS focuses on general text or specific task completion, rather than identifying functional equivalence *during reasoning*. The generation of *MathEquiv*, a specialized dataset for mathematical statement equivalence, is a *clear* novel contribution. Existing STS datasets generally don't focus on capturing mathematical functional equivalences accurately.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to address a major bottleneck in LLM-based reasoning: computational cost. By reducing token consumption (as demonstrated by the significant reductions reported), EquivPruner can make complex reasoning tasks more accessible and scalable. The improvements in accuracy, even if marginal in some cases, further enhance the method's value. A very high baseline accuracy makes it very difficult to improve on, so even a slight increase is worthwhile. The open-sourcing of MathEquiv is significant, as it offers a valuable resource for future research in this area. The concept can be applied in a variety of other fields, however it is limited by the need for task and reward-specific fine-tuning of the models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of redundant exploration in LLM search.
    *   **Effective Approach:** EquivPruner provides a straightforward and effective solution with demonstrable results.
    *   **Novel Dataset:** The MathEquiv dataset fills a gap in existing resources for mathematical reasoning.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across different models and datasets support the claims.
    *   **Generalizability:** Demonstrations of effectiveness on OOD models and datasets strengthens the claim of generalizability.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study clearly validates the design decisions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Scale:** The evaluation is primarily conducted on models up to 7B parameters. While this is understandable due to computational constraints, it would be beneficial to see how EquivPruner scales to larger models. The larger the base LLM is, the greater the computational savings that are observed by using EquivPruner.
    *   **Mathematical Focus:** While the framework is potentially generalizable, the primary focus is on mathematical reasoning. Demonstrating effectiveness in other domains would further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Performance ceiling:** The performance is capped by the quality of reward function. This limits the impact that EquivPruner can have on accuracy.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the design of more efficient LLM-based reasoning systems. EquivPruner's approach could be integrated into existing search algorithms, and the MathEquiv dataset could be used to train more robust equivalence detectors. It also highlights the importance of considering semantic redundancy in LLM outputs and developing strategies to mitigate its impact.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a moderately novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM-based reasoning. The EquivPruner approach effectively addresses the problem of redundant exploration, leading to substantial reductions in token consumption and improved accuracy. The creation of the MathEquiv dataset is also a valuable contribution. However, the limited model scale and domain-specific focus somewhat constrain the paper's overall impact. Nonetheless, the work is well-executed, clearly presented, and has the potential to influence future research in this area.
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### NTIRE 2025 challenge on Text to Image Generation Model Quality Assessment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16314v1)
- **Authors**: Shuhao Han, Haotian Fan, Fangyuan Kong, Wenjie Liao, Chunle Guo, Chongyi Li, Radu Timofte, Liang Li, Tao Li, Junhui Cui, Yunqiu Wang, Yang Tai, Jingwei Sun, Jianhui Sun, Xinli Yue, Tianyi Wang, Huan Hou, Junda Lu, Xinyang Huang, Zitang Zhou, Zijian Zhang, Xuhui Zheng, Xuecheng Wu, Chong Peng, Xuezhi Cao, Trong-Hieu Nguyen-Mau, Minh-Hoang Le, Minh-Khoa Le-Phan, Duy-Nam Ly, Hai-Dang Nguyen, Minh-Triet Tran, Yukang Lin, Yan Hong, Chuanbiao Song, Siyuan Li, Jun Lan, Zhichao Zhang, Xinyue Li, Wei Sun, Zicheng Zhang, Yunhao Li, Xiaohong Liu, Guangtao Zhai, Zitong Xu, Huiyu Duan, Jiarui Wang, Guangji Ma, Liu Yang, Lu Liu, Qiang Hu, Xiongkuo Min, Zichuan Wang, Zhenchen Tang, Bo Peng, Jing Dong, Fengbin Guan, Zihao Yu, Yiting Lu, Wei Luo, Xin Li, Minhao Lin, Haofeng Chen, Xuanxuan He, Kele Xu, Qisheng Xu, Zijian Gao, Tianjiao Wan, Bo-Cheng Qiu, Chih-Chung Hsu, Chia-ming Lee, Yu-Fan Lin, Bo Yu, Zehao Wang, Da Mu, Mingxiu Chen, Junkang Fang, Huamei Sun, Wending Zhao, Zhiyu Wang, Wang Liu, Weikang Yu, Puhong Duan, Bin Sun, Xudong Kang, Shutao Li, Shuai He, Lingzhi Fu, Heng Cong, Rongyu Zhang, Jiarong He, Zhishan Qiao, Yongqing Huang, Zewen Chen, Zhe Pang, Juan Wang, Jian Guo, Zhizhuo Shao, Ziyu Feng, Bing Li, Weiming Hu, Hesong Li, Dehua Liu, Zeming Liu, Qingsong Xie, Ruichen Wang, Zhihao Li, Yuqi Liang, Jianqi Bi, Jun Luo, Junfeng Yang, Can Li, Jing Fu, Hongwei Xu, Mingrui Long, Lulin Tang
- **Abstract**: This paper reports on the NTIRE 2025 challenge on Text to Image (T2I) generation model quality assessment, which will be held in conjunction with the New Trends in Image Restoration and Enhancement Workshop (NTIRE) at CVPR 2025. The aim of this challenge is to address the fine-grained quality assessment of text-to-image generation models. This challenge evaluates text-to-image models from two aspects: image-text alignment and image structural distortion detection, and is divided into the alignment track and the structural track. The alignment track uses the EvalMuse-40K, which contains around 40K AI-Generated Images (AIGIs) generated by 20 popular generative models. The alignment track has a total of 371 registered participants. A total of 1,883 submissions are received in the development phase, and 507 submissions are received in the test phase. Finally, 12 participating teams submitted their models and fact sheets. The structure track uses the EvalMuse-Structure, which contains 10,000 AI-Generated Images (AIGIs) with corresponding structural distortion mask. A total of 211 participants have registered in the structure track. A total of 1155 submissions are received in the development phase, and 487 submissions are received in the test phase. Finally, 8 participating teams submitted their models and fact sheets. Almost all methods have achieved better results than baseline methods, and the winning methods in both tracks have demonstrated superior prediction performance on T2I model quality assessment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the NTIRE 2025 challenge paper on Text-to-Image Generation Model Quality Assessment:

**Summary:**

The paper describes the NTIRE 2025 challenge focused on evaluating the quality of text-to-image (T2I) generation models. The challenge is split into two tracks: an alignment track, assessing how well the generated images match the input text, and a structural track, evaluating the authenticity and fidelity of the image structure by detecting distortions.  The alignment track uses the EvalMuse-40K dataset with fine-grained annotations, while the structure track introduces a new dataset, EvalMuse-Structure, containing images and structural distortion masks. The paper details the datasets, evaluation protocols (SRCC, PLCC, Accuracy, F1 Score), challenge phases (development and testing), and provides a summary of the participating teams and their methods. The results show that participating teams generally outperform baseline methods in both tracks, demonstrating progress in fine-grained quality assessment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the combination of several aspects:

*   **Fine-grained focus:** The challenge's explicit goal of assessing fine-grained alignment and structural fidelity is a valuable contribution.
*   **EvalMuse-Structure Dataset:** The introduction of a new dataset with structural distortion masks is a novel resource for the community.  Datasets specifically designed for structural evaluation in T2I are less common than those focusing on alignment or overall quality.
*   **Challenge format:**  While individual IQA methods exist, organizing a challenge to benchmark and compare approaches is beneficial for driving progress and highlighting effective techniques.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing a key problem:**  The paper addresses a fundamental issue in T2I research: how to reliably and accurately evaluate generated images. As T2I models become more powerful, robust evaluation metrics are crucial for guiding further improvements.
*   **Benchmarking diverse approaches:**  The NTIRE challenge provides a platform to compare a wide range of IQA methods, which helps to identify the strengths and weaknesses of different techniques (e.g., those using MLLMs vs. those employing dedicated image analysis).
*   **Potential impact:**  By pushing the development of better IQA metrics, the challenge contributes to creating more controllable and reliable T2I models. This can have a broad impact on various applications of T2I technology.
*   **Community building:**  Challenges like this help to bring together researchers working on related problems, which fosters collaboration and knowledge sharing.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental progress:** The methods used by participating teams often build upon existing IQA techniques (e.g., using MLLMs with fine-tuning or adapting existing architectures). While improvements over the baseline are demonstrated, the degree of innovation in *individual* participating methods may vary significantly and is not deeply explored. The description of the specific methods remain brief.
*   **Dataset dependence:** The challenge's reliance on the EvalMuse datasets, while providing fine-grained annotations, may also introduce dataset-specific biases.  It's important to consider how well the methods generalize to other T2I datasets. This isn't thoroughly addressed within the paper.
*   **Limited insights on failure cases:** The paper provides a general overview of the results, but it does not go into detail about the specific types of alignment or structural distortions that are still challenging for current IQA methods. Understanding these failure cases is crucial for directing future research.
*   **Limited discussion of limitations of the evaluation protocol:** While the paper introduces evaluation metrics such as SRCC, PLCC, ACC, and F1 score, it lacks a thorough discussion of the limitations of these metrics, such as potential biases, sensitivity to specific distortion types, or the inherent subjectivity of human perception.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the aspects discussed above, the paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of text-to-image generation. The NTIRE challenge provides a valuable platform for benchmarking T2I quality assessment methods and introduces a useful new dataset (EvalMuse-Structure). However, it is somewhat incremental in terms of the novelty of individual methods, and lacks detailed analysis of limitations and potential dataset biases. Therefore, a high but not extremely high score is warranted.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### TensorAR: Refinement is All You Need in Autoregressive Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16324v1)
- **Authors**: Cheng Cheng, Lin Song, Yicheng Xiao, Yuxin Chen, Xuchong Zhang, Hongbin Sun, Ying Shan
- **Abstract**: Autoregressive (AR) image generators offer a language-model-friendly approach to image generation by predicting discrete image tokens in a causal sequence. However, unlike diffusion models, AR models lack a mechanism to refine previous predictions, limiting their generation quality. In this paper, we introduce TensorAR, a new AR paradigm that reformulates image generation from next-token prediction to next-tensor prediction. By generating overlapping windows of image patches (tensors) in a sliding fashion, TensorAR enables iterative refinement of previously generated content. To prevent information leakage during training, we propose a discrete tensor noising scheme, which perturbs input tokens via codebook-indexed noise. TensorAR is implemented as a plug-and-play module compatible with existing AR models. Extensive experiments on LlamaGEN, Open-MAGVIT2, and RAR demonstrate that TensorAR significantly improves the generation performance of autoregressive models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TensorAR, a novel autoregressive (AR) image generation paradigm that aims to refine previously generated image patches, addressing a limitation of traditional AR models which lack a mechanism for iterative improvement. TensorAR achieves this by predicting overlapping "tensors" (windows of image patches) in a sliding fashion, allowing later predictions to revise earlier ones.  To avoid information leakage during training, the authors propose a discrete tensor noising scheme that perturbs input tokens using codebook-indexed noise. TensorAR is designed as a plug-and-play module compatible with existing AR models like LlamaGEN, Open-MAGVIT2, and RAR. Experimental results demonstrate that TensorAR improves the generation performance of these models, achieving better image quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of refining previous image patches within an autoregressive framework is a conceptually novel contribution.  While techniques like "coarse-to-fine" generation exist, the specific approach of using overlapping tensors and a discrete noise schedule within an AR model seems unique.  The plug-and-play aspect is a practical and clever design choice.
* **Significance:** The paper's potential significance lies in its ability to bridge the gap between diffusion models (known for high-quality images and refinement) and AR models (which are more compatible with large language models). Improving AR image generation has implications for multimodal generation and controllable image synthesis.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of standard AR models compared to diffusion models and proposes a targeted solution.
    * **Elegant Solution:**  The TensorAR approach is conceptually elegant and relatively simple to implement as a plug-in module. The discrete noise scheme is a clever way to address information leakage.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments show consistent performance improvements across various base AR models (LlamaGen, Open-MAGVIT2, and RAR). The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of different components.
    * **Well-Written:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, explaining the motivation, approach, and results clearly.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the improvements are consistent, they are not overwhelmingly large compared to existing AR and diffusion models.  The gains are more in the realm of refinement than revolution. While the performance nears diffusion models, it doesn't surpass it.
    * **Limited Evaluation:**  The primary evaluation focuses on ImageNet, a standard but somewhat constrained dataset. Further testing on more complex and diverse datasets is needed to fully assess the robustness and generalizability of TensorAR. While ImageNet represents a foundational dataset, performance can often be skewed by dataset-specific tuning.
    * **Computational Cost:**  Although the plug-in nature minimizes the impact of additional parameters, the overlapping windows inherently increase computational cost due to increased tensor size. The experiments do analyze throughput, but a more thorough exploration of the trade-off between quality and speed would be beneficial.
* **Impact:**
    * TensorAR's plug-and-play nature is a positive factor. It is likely to influence research in AR image generation by encouraging exploration of refinement mechanisms. It could potentially be adopted by researchers and practitioners looking to improve the quality of their AR-based image synthesis pipelines without drastically changing their existing models.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid, novel contribution to the field of AR image generation by introducing a refinement mechanism. While the improvements are not groundbreaking, they are consistent and achieved with an elegant and practical approach. The plug-and-play design and clear writing enhance its potential impact. However, the somewhat incremental nature of the performance gains and the limited scope of the evaluation (mostly ImageNet) prevent it from receiving a higher score. The 7 reflects a significant, but not transformative, addition to the existing literature, characterized by well-reasoned designs, sound methodology, and the potential to impact future research directions.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ChemMLLM: Chemical Multimodal Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16326v1)
- **Authors**: Qian Tan, Dongzhan Zhou, Peng Xia, Wanhao Liu, Wanli Ouyang, Lei Bai, Yuqiang Li, Tianfan Fu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made impressive progress in many applications in recent years. However, chemical MLLMs that can handle cross-modal understanding and generation remain underexplored. To fill this gap, in this paper, we propose ChemMLLM, a unified chemical multimodal large language model for molecule understanding and generation. Also, we design five multimodal tasks across text, molecular SMILES strings, and image, and curate the datasets.
