We benchmark ChemMLLM against a range of general leading MLLMs and Chemical LLMs on these tasks. Experimental results show that ChemMLLM achieves superior performance across all evaluated tasks. For example, in molecule image optimization task, ChemMLLM outperforms the best baseline (GPT-4o) by 118.9\% (4.27 vs 1.95 property improvement). The code is publicly available at https://github.com/bbsbz/ChemMLLM.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChemMLLM, a chemical multimodal large language model designed to understand and generate molecules across text, SMILES strings, and molecular images. The authors address the lack of integrated chemical MLLMs by creating a unified framework that supports both multimodal understanding and generation. This involves identifying five new multimodal chemistry tasks with three modalities (text, SMILES, image), fine-tuning a molecule image-level VQGAN to bridge the gap between molecule and natural images, and introducing an "Image Tokenizer-LLM-Image De-tokenizer" architecture to fuse modalities and enable image generation. The paper benchmarks ChemMLLM against existing general and chemical LLMs, demonstrating superior performance on various tasks. A two-stage training strategy and its effectiveness are also highlighted.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach by integrating multimodal capabilities (text, SMILES, image) into a single chemical LLM. Existing chemical MLLMs primarily focus on understanding tasks or treat images as inputs only, whereas ChemMLLM introduces image generation capabilities, making it more versatile. The introduction of the "Image Tokenizer-LLM-Image De-tokenizer" architecture tailored for chemical tasks is another novel aspect. Defining five new multimodal chemistry tasks provides a useful benchmark for future research.

*   **Significance:** The ability to generate molecular images directly from chemical language or structure has significant implications for chemists' communication and reasoning. It allows for more intuitive and visual interactions with AI, which could greatly expand the applications of MLLMs in chemistry. The superior performance of ChemMLLM over existing models highlights the importance of domain-specific adaptation and multimodal integration. By providing a unified framework and benchmark datasets, the paper contributes to the development of more sophisticated chemical AI tools. The improvement in the molecule image optimization task (118.9% improvement over GPT-40) further validates its significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** ChemMLLM addresses the key challenges in building chemical MLLMs and offers a well-defined architecture and training strategy.
    *   **Multimodal Integration:** Seamlessly fuses text, SMILES strings, and images, enabling both understanding and generation tasks.
    *   **New Datasets and Tasks:** The introduction of five new multimodal chemistry tasks and datasets contributes to the field.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** Benchmarking against existing models demonstrates ChemMLLM's superior performance.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the methodology, experiments, and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Modalities:** The model only incorporates text, SMILES strings, and 2D molecule images. Real-world chemical data often includes richer modalities such as 3D molecular structures, quantum mechanical properties, or spectroscopic data.
    *   **Task Focus:** The evaluation is primarily focused on proof-of-concept chemistry tasks. Further studies are needed to validate the model's performance in real-world applications such as drug discovery, materials design, or chemical synthesis planning.
    *   **Reliance on Existing VQGAN:** While fine-tuning VQGAN is beneficial, a complete end-to-end training might provide greater flexibility and potentially better performance. The paper mentions the challenges of vision-based chemistry tasks, but a more extensive analysis and handling of these challenges could add further depth.

*   **Potential Influence:** ChemMLLM's architecture and training methodology could serve as a blueprint for future chemical MLLMs. The benchmark datasets and tasks can be used to evaluate new models and drive further advancements. The ability to generate molecular images from various modalities could have practical applications in drug discovery and materials design. The modular architecture of the model lends itself to potential future expansion into more modalities.

*   **Justification:** While ChemMLLM exhibits notable advancements in chemical multimodal learning by successfully integrating text, SMILES, and image data, its real-world applicability remains to be fully validated. The integration of additional modalities such as 3D structures or spectra would enable it to capture more complex molecular interactions. The performance of ChemMLLM in real-world applications such as drug discovery and materials design requires further study. The challenges in developing vision-based chemistry tasks needs to be further examined and addressed. The significant improvement and novel framework design merit recognition, but it's not without its limitations.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SC4ANM: Identifying Optimal Section Combinations for Automated Novelty Prediction in Academic Papers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16330v1)
- **Authors**: Wenqing Wu, Chengzhi Zhang, Tong Bao, Yi Zhao
- **Abstract**: Novelty is a core component of academic papers, and there are multiple perspectives on the assessment of novelty. Existing methods often focus on word or entity combinations, which provide limited insights. The content related to a paper's novelty is typically distributed across different core sections, e.g., Introduction, Methodology and Results. Therefore, exploring the optimal combination of sections for evaluating the novelty of a paper is important for advancing automated novelty assessment. In this paper, we utilize different combinations of sections from academic papers as inputs to drive language models to predict novelty scores. We then analyze the results to determine the optimal section combinations for novelty score prediction. We first employ natural language processing techniques to identify the sectional structure of academic papers, categorizing them into introduction, methods, results, and discussion (IMRaD). Subsequently, we used different combinations of these sections (e.g., introduction and methods) as inputs for pretrained language models (PLMs) and large language models (LLMs), employing novelty scores provided by human expert reviewers as ground truth labels to obtain prediction results. The results indicate that using introduction, results and discussion is most appropriate for assessing the novelty of a paper, while the use of the entire text does not yield significant results. Furthermore, based on the results of the PLMs and LLMs, the introduction and results appear to be the most important section for the task of novelty score prediction. The code and dataset for this paper can be accessed at https://github.com/njust-winchy/SC4ANM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SC4ANM: Identifying Optimal Section Combinations for Automated Novelty Prediction in Academic Papers" investigates which combinations of sections in academic papers (Introduction, Methods, Results, Discussion - IMRaD) are most effective for automated novelty prediction.  The authors fine-tune pre-trained language models (PLMs) and utilize large language models (LLMs) with various IMRaD section combinations as input, using expert reviewers' novelty scores as ground truth.  The study aims to determine the best section combinations for accurately predicting novelty scores and analyzes the contributions of individual sections. They found that a combination of Introduction, Results, and Discussion sections (IRD) is optimal for novelty assessment using PLMs and LLMs and that using PLMs performed better overall.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a gap in the existing literature on automated novelty assessment. While prior works often focus on analyzing citations, keywords, or abstracts, this paper investigates the predictive power of different core text sections within an academic paper. The explicit focus on IMRaD structure combinations to emulate the cognitive process during peer review is novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to improve the efficiency and accuracy of automated peer review systems. By identifying which sections are most informative for novelty assessment, these systems can prioritize the analysis of those sections, leading to better results. The study contributes to a deeper understanding of how experts evaluate novelty based on different textual components.

*   **Methodology:** The methodology is reasonably sound.
    *   The authors use a standard IMRaD structure as a segmentation framework. While not all papers adhere strictly to this structure, the authors use NLP techniques to identify section boundaries.
    *   The choice of PLMs (Longformer, BigBird, etc.) is appropriate due to their ability to handle longer sequences.
    *   The use of human reviewer scores as ground truth is justified.
    *   The evaluation metrics (Accuracy, F1-score, Pearson, Spearman, and Kendall's tau correlation coefficients) are standard for classification and regression tasks.
    *   A weakness is the relatively small scale of LLM tests and the random sampling data procedure.

*   **Results and Discussion:** The results are clearly presented, and the analysis is generally well-reasoned.
    *   The conclusion that Introduction, Results, and Discussion sections are most effective is supported by the data.
    *   The comparison with a traditional citation-based novelty assessment method provides further validation of their approach.
    *   However, the low performance of LLMs, with the highest accuracy reaching only 0.4, is a significant drawback.
    *   The reliance on GPT API makes the experiment reproducible, yet dependent on external service.

*   **Limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their study. The reliance on computer science papers from a single conference (ICLR) limits the generalizability of the findings. The authors also point out the challenges in capturing the full complexity of human judgment involved in novelty assessment. They did not account for other content from academic papers, such as tables, figures and charts.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to automated novelty assessment by exploring the role of different section combinations in academic papers. While the methodology has limitations, the findings offer practical insights for improving the design of automated peer review systems, which could be useful for fields beyond computer science. The work paves the way for more sophisticated approaches to simulating human reviewer behavior.

Score: 7

**Rationale:**

The paper's novelty lies in its focus on section combinations and its attempt to emulate the expert review process through AI. The potential impact is significant as it could improve the quality and efficiency of peer review. However, some weaknesses prevent a higher score:

*   Limited dataset (single conference, computer science domain)
*   Relatively poor performance of LLMs compared to PLMs
*   Dependence on the IMRaD structure, which may not be universally applicable.
*   Small scale LLM experimentation and random sampling for experimental data

The score reflects the paper's strong potential impact and reasonable methodology but acknowledges its limitations in generalizability and LLM performance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Panoptic Captioning: Seeking An Equivalency Bridge for Image and Text
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16334v1)
- **Authors**: Kun-Yu Lin, Hongjun Wang, Weining Ren, Kai Han
- **Abstract**: This work introduces panoptic captioning, a novel task striving to seek the minimum text equivalence of images. We take the first step towards panoptic captioning by formulating it as a task of generating a comprehensive textual description for an image, which encapsulates all entities, their respective locations and attributes, relationships among entities, as well as global image state.Through an extensive evaluation, our work reveals that state-of-the-art Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have limited performance in solving panoptic captioning. To address this, we propose an effective data engine named PancapEngine to produce high-quality data and a novel method named PancapChain to improve panoptic captioning. Specifically, our PancapEngine first detects diverse categories of entities in images by an elaborate detection suite, and then generates required panoptic captions using entity-aware prompts. Additionally, our PancapChain explicitly decouples the challenging panoptic captioning task into multiple stages and generates panoptic captions step by step. More importantly, we contribute a comprehensive metric named PancapScore and a human-curated test set for reliable model evaluation.Experiments show that our PancapChain-13B model can beat state-of-the-art open-source MLLMs like InternVL-2.5-78B and even surpass proprietary models like GPT-4o and Gemini-2.0-Pro, demonstrating the effectiveness of our data engine and method. Project page: https://visual-ai.github.io/pancap/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Panoptic Captioning: Seeking An Equivalency Bridge for Image and Text" paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces the task of "panoptic captioning," aiming to create a concise textual representation of an image that captures all entities, their locations, attributes, relationships, and the overall image state. The authors frame this as seeking the "minimum text equivalence" of an image.  The paper proposes:

1.  A formulation of panoptic captioning as a task involving these five dimensions.
2.  PancapEngine, a data engine to generate high-quality training data based on detection-then-caption.
3.  SA-Pancap, a benchmark dataset for the task, including a human-curated test set.
4.  PancapChain, a multi-stage method to improve panoptic captioning by breaking the task into entity localization, tagging, and caption generation.
5.  PancapScore, a new metric designed to evaluate the performance of models on panoptic captioning by independently scoring the tagging, localization, attribute, relation, and global state dimensions.

The paper demonstrates that existing MLLMs struggle with this task.  Experiments show the proposed PancapChain model, particularly PancapChain-13B, outperforms various state-of-the-art models, including larger, proprietary models, on the SA-Pancap benchmark.  It also shows promise for downstream tasks like image-text retrieval.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of panoptic captioning is novel and addresses a real gap in current image captioning research. The problem formulation, encompassing the five dimensions (tagging, location, attributes, relations, and global state), is a valuable contribution. The PancapScore metric is also a significant advance as it provides a way to evaluate comprehensive image representations which traditional captioning metrics cannot address. Finally, the staged PanchapChain offers an architectural insight to handle such complex descriptions.
*   **Significance:** The paper highlights the limitations of existing MLLMs for creating truly comprehensive image representations and provides a tangible pathway to improve them. The SA-Pancap benchmark is a useful resource for the community. The ability to accurately capture all entities and their relations has many potential applications, including improved cross-modal understanding and downstream tasks. The reported improvement in image-text retrieval, although preliminary, suggests value in the approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines a new and important task.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The introduction of the PancapScore metric is crucial for assessing performance on the proposed task. The experiments are thorough, comparing against multiple SOTA methods and different prompts.
    *   **Methodology:** The PancapEngine addresses a real need for high-quality panoptic captions data. The multi-stage PancapChain offers a tangible approach to decomposing the complex captioning.
    *   **Open Source Contribution:** The publication of the dataset and code allows for reproduction of experiments and further studies.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Definition of "Minimum Text Equivalence":** While the paper frames its goal as finding the minimum text equivalence, the current formulation, while comprehensive, still seems more detailed than a truly "minimum" representation.
    *   **Reliance on Automatic Data Generation:**  Although the human curated test set is valuable, the primary training data depends on the output of PancapEngine. Errors in automatic data generation could affect the model's performance and limit its generalization capabilities. The paper could benefit from a more detailed error analysis of the PancapEngine. While caption consistency helps, potential biases or inaccuracies will still pass through.
    *   **Downstream Applications:** The results for downstream image-text retrieval tasks, while promising, are relatively modest. The paper could benefit from more substantial evaluation on other downstream applications to demonstrate the practical impact of panoptic captioning.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't extensively discuss the computational cost of the PancapChain model compared to simpler captioning approaches. While the model is 13B, the multi-stage nature could lead to increased compute.
*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to significantly influence research in image captioning, visual understanding, and cross-modal learning. The SA-Pancap benchmark will likely become a standard for evaluating models on comprehensive image understanding.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution, defining a novel task and providing a framework (data, method, and metric) for addressing it. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of the proposed approach over existing methods. While the concept of "minimum text equivalence" and the reliance on auto-generated data could be improved, the overall quality of the research is high. Therefore, a score of 8 reflects the paper's significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### FPQVAR: Floating Point Quantization for Visual Autoregressive Model with FPGA Hardware Co-design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16335v1)
- **Authors**: Renjie Wei, Songqiang Xu, Qingyu Guo, Meng Li
- **Abstract**: Visual autoregressive (VAR) modeling has marked a paradigm shift in image generation from next-token prediction to next-scale prediction. VAR predicts a set of tokens at each step from coarse to fine scale, leading to better image quality and faster inference speed compared to existing diffusion models. However, the large parameter size and computation cost hinder its deployment on edge devices. To reduce the memory and computation cost, we propose FPQVAR, an efficient post-training floating-point (FP) quantization framework for VAR featuring algorithm and hardware co-design. At the algorithm level, we first identify the challenges of quantizing VAR. To address them, we propose Dual Format Quantization for the highly imbalanced input activation. We further propose Group-wise Hadamard Transformation and GHT-Aware Learnable Transformation to address the time-varying outlier channels. At the hardware level, we design the first low-bit FP quantizer and multiplier with lookup tables on FPGA and propose the first FPGA-based VAR accelerator featuring low-bit FP computation and an elaborate two-level pipeline. Extensive experiments show that compared to the state-of-the-art quantization method, our proposed FPQVAR significantly improves Fr\'echet Inception Distance (FID) from 10.83 to 3.58, Inception Score (IS) from 175.9 to 241.5 under 4-bit quantization. FPQVAR also significantly improves the performance of 6-bit quantized VAR, bringing it on par with the FP16 model. Our accelerator on AMD-Xilinx VCK190 FPGA achieves a throughput of 1.1 image/s, which is 3.1x higher than the integer-based accelerator. It also demonstrates 3.6x and 2.8x higher energy efficiency compared to the integer-based accelerator and GPU baseline, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FPQVAR: Floating Point Quantization for Visual Autoregressive Model with FPGA Hardware Co-design":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FPQVAR, a post-training floating-point (FP) quantization framework specifically designed for Visual Autoregressive (VAR) models and optimized for FPGA implementation. It addresses challenges in quantizing VAR models, including imbalanced activation distributions and time-varying outlier channels. The key contributions are:

1.  **Dual Format Quantization (DFQ):**  Handles imbalanced activation distributions by using different FP formats for positive and negative values.
2.  **Group-wise Hadamard Transformation (GHT) and GHT-Aware Learnable Transformation (GALT):** Mitigates time-varying outlier channels by applying Hadamard transforms group-wise and learning adaptive smoothing factors.
3.  **FPGA Hardware Co-design:**  Designs an efficient low-bit FP quantizer and multiplier for FPGA, leading to a VAR accelerator with low-bit FP computation and a two-level pipeline.

Experimental results show significant improvements in Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) and Inception Score (IS) compared to existing quantization methods and demonstrate higher throughput and energy efficiency on an FPGA platform compared to integer-based accelerators and GPUs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important problem: reducing the computational cost and memory footprint of VAR models for deployment on resource-constrained devices. VAR models, while promising for image generation, are computationally demanding. The paper's approach of using floating-point quantization and hardware co-design to address this challenge is significant. However, the level of novelty and long-term impact deserves critical analysis.

**Strengths:**

*   **Targeted Problem:** Directly tackles the challenges of quantizing VAR models, which are distinct from those encountered with other generative models like diffusion models.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:**  Combines algorithm-level optimizations (DFQ, GHT, GALT) with hardware-level design, which is crucial for realizing practical benefits.
*   **Significant Performance Gains:** Demonstrates substantial improvements in FID and IS, especially at low bit-widths (4-bit), indicating effective compression without significant quality loss.
*   **FPGA Implementation:**  Provides a detailed FPGA design, including the low-bit FP quantizer and multiplier, showcasing the feasibility and efficiency of the proposed approach. The FPGA results showing speedup and energy efficiency gains over both integer-based FPGA and GPU implementations are compelling.
*   **Clear Explanations and Ablation Studies:**  Provides clear explanations of the proposed techniques and includes ablation studies to evaluate the contribution of individual components.
*   **Well-written and structured paper:** The paper is well-organized and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of techniques is novel, each individual component (DFQ, GHT, GALT) builds upon existing ideas from quantization literature. DFQ resembles asymmetric quantization methods; GHT uses Hadamard transforms, and GALT uses learnable scales.  The novelty lies in *adapting* these techniques to the *specific challenges of VAR* and *co-designing with FPGA hardware*.  How much the authors innovated the individual techniques needs further reflection.
*   **Limited Scope of VAR Models:** The results are primarily demonstrated on one specific VAR model.  Generalizability to other VAR architectures needs to be better substantiated.  Are the identified outlier channel issues consistently present in all VAR variants?
*   **Complexity:** The combined algorithm is relatively complex, incorporating multiple stages of quantization and transformation. Although the steps are explained clearly, it's more difficult to implement compared to a single quantization technique. This could limit adoption.
*   **Dependence on specific hardware**: Given that it's a codesign approach, the paper does not explore the efficacy of the quantization techniques independently of the custom hardware.
*   **Limited Comparison with SOTA Hardware:** Although the comparison with GPU is provided, a better comparison would've been with state-of-the-art, custom built accelerators that optimize specifically for LLMs/Diffusion Models

**Significance:**

The work has the potential to enable wider adoption of VAR models by making them more practical for deployment on edge devices. The hardware co-design approach is particularly valuable as it considers the constraints and capabilities of FPGA platforms. The performance gains achieved by FPQVAR are significant enough to warrant further research and development in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid engineering contribution, effectively addressing the quantization challenges of VAR models and achieving significant performance improvements through algorithm and hardware co-design. However, the algorithmic novelty is incremental, with the main contribution being the adaptation of existing techniques to the specific characteristics of VAR. The focus is mainly on demonstrating an FPGA implementation, with lesser regard given to benchmarking with SOTA methods. Therefore, a score of 7 is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Chemical Understanding of LLMs via SMILES Parsing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16340v1)
- **Authors**: Yunhui Jang, Jaehyung Kim, Sungsoo Ahn
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly recognized as powerful tools for scientific discovery, particularly in molecular science. A fundamental requirement for these models is the ability to accurately understand molecular structures, commonly encoded in the SMILES representation. However, current LLMs struggle to interpret SMILES, even failing to carry out basic tasks such as counting molecular rings. To address this limitation, we introduce CLEANMOL, a novel framework that formulates SMILES parsing into a suite of clean and deterministic tasks explicitly designed to promote graph-level molecular comprehension. These tasks span from subgraph matching to global graph matching, providing structured supervision aligned with molecular structural properties. We construct a molecular pretraining dataset with adaptive difficulty scoring and pre-train open-source LLMs on these tasks. Our results show that CLEANMOL not only enhances structural comprehension but also achieves the best or competes with the baseline on the Mol-Instructions benchmark.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper identifies a crucial limitation in current Large Language Models (LLMs) when applied to molecular science: their difficulty in accurately interpreting SMILES strings, a standard representation of molecular structures.  To address this, the authors introduce CLEANMOL, a novel framework that frames SMILES parsing as a series of clean, deterministic tasks. These tasks, ranging from subgraph matching to global graph matching, provide structured supervision to enhance graph-level molecular comprehension. They create a new pretraining dataset (CLEANMOL dataset) using these tasks and pretrain open-source LLMs. The results demonstrate improved structural comprehension and competitive or state-of-the-art performance on the Mol-Instructions benchmark. The paper further details task-adaptive data pruning and curriculum learning strategies to boost performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its explicit framing of SMILES parsing as a suite of structured, deterministic tasks and the creation of a dataset specifically designed for this purpose. While LLMs for chemistry are not new, and supervised fine-tuning is a known technique, the targeted, structured approach to pretraining on SMILES parsing tasks is a relatively fresh idea. The task-adaptive data pruning and curriculum learning strategies further enhance the novelty.

**Significance:** The significance stems from directly addressing a recognized bottleneck in the application of LLMs to chemistry.  By improving the ability of LLMs to accurately understand and parse SMILES strings, the framework enhances their potential for various downstream tasks, including molecular generation, retrosynthetic analysis, and drug discovery. The empirical results on Mol-Instructions demonstrate a tangible improvement, suggesting that this structural understanding is crucial for enhancing general capabilities on more complex chemical tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper pinpoints a specific weakness of current LLMs in molecular science, namely, the poor understanding of SMILES strings and their underlying graph representation.
*   **Well-Structured Approach:** CLEANMOL offers a well-defined, modular framework consisting of distinct SMILES parsing tasks, a dedicated dataset, and a two-stage training procedure.
*   **Deterministic and Scalable Tasks:** The choice of deterministic tasks is a significant strength, ensuring unambiguous supervision and facilitating reliable learning. The scalability is crucial, enabling the framework to leverage large amounts of unlabeled molecular data.
*   **Strong Empirical Validation:** The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of CLEANMOL through comprehensive experiments, showcasing improved performance on SMILES parsing and downstream molecular tasks compared to baseline and state-of-the-art models.
*   **Reproducibility:** The code release and clear documentation enhance reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Structural Information:** The framework primarily focuses on 2D graph-level understanding and does not incorporate 3D information. While understandable given the chosen focus, this limits the scope of the framework in applications where 3D conformation is critical.
*   **Dependency on RDKit:** The reliance on RDKit for annotation, while practical, introduces a dependency and potential bottleneck.  The generated annotations' quality directly relies on RDKit's capabilities.
*   **Dataset Scale:** While the paper claims scalability, the actual dataset size (250K molecules) is relatively small compared to datasets commonly used in other domains of LLM research. Further scaling would likely improve the performance and robustness of the framework.
*   **Limited Model Scale:** The work primarily explores models with 7-8B parameters, which is appropriate but doesn't demonstrate the behavior on truly large language models (e.g., models > 50B parameters).

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   **Promoting explicit structure-aware pretraining:** It encourages a shift towards explicitly addressing structural understanding rather than relying on implicit learning from large molecular corpora.
*   **Guiding the development of future molecular LLMs:** The well-defined SMILES parsing tasks can serve as a benchmark and guide for evaluating and improving the structural understanding capabilities of future models.
*   **Facilitating integration with other techniques:** CLEANMOL can be combined with existing methods such as instruction tuning and domain adaptation to create more robust and versatile molecular LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by directly addressing a known limitation of LLMs in molecular science. The CLEANMOL framework provides a structured and scalable approach to enhance structural understanding through SMILES parsing. The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements in both parsing and downstream tasks. However, the limitations related to dataset size, model scale, and the lack of 3D information prevent a higher score. The paper's impact could be increased through further research addressing these weaknesses and exploring its integration with complementary techniques. The novelty is good but not groundbreaking, and the significance is tied to improvements in existing models and tasks.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Embodied Agents Meet Personalization: Exploring Memory Utilization for Personalized Assistance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16348v1)
- **Authors**: Taeyoon Kwon, Dongwook Choi, Sunghwan Kim, Hyojun Kim, Seungjun Moon, Beong-woo Kwak, Kuan-Hao Huang, Jinyoung Yeo
- **Abstract**: Embodied agents empowered by large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance in household object rearrangement tasks. However, these tasks primarily focus on single-turn interactions with simplified instructions, which do not truly reflect the challenges of providing meaningful assistance to users. To provide personalized assistance, embodied agents must understand the unique semantics that users assign to the physical world (e.g., favorite cup, breakfast routine) by leveraging prior interaction history to interpret dynamic, real-world instructions. Yet, the effectiveness of embodied agents in utilizing memory for personalized assistance remains largely underexplored. To address this gap, we present MEMENTO, a personalized embodied agent evaluation framework designed to comprehensively assess memory utilization capabilities to provide personalized assistance. Our framework consists of a two-stage memory evaluation process design that enables quantifying the impact of memory utilization on task performance. This process enables the evaluation of agents' understanding of personalized knowledge in object rearrangement tasks by focusing on its role in goal interpretation: (1) the ability to identify target objects based on personal meaning (object semantics), and (2) the ability to infer object-location configurations from consistent user patterns, such as routines (user patterns). Our experiments across various LLMs reveal significant limitations in memory utilization, with even frontier models like GPT-4o experiencing a 30.5% performance drop when required to reference multiple memories, particularly in tasks involving user patterns. These findings, along with our detailed analyses and case studies, provide valuable insights for future research in developing more effective personalized embodied agents. Project website: https://connoriginal.github.io/MEMENTO
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MEMENTO, a novel framework for evaluating how well embodied agents utilize memory (specifically episodic memory) to provide personalized assistance.  It emphasizes the importance of agents understanding user-specific semantics and patterns to interpret instructions effectively. MEMENTO consists of two stages: Memory Acquisition, where the agent learns user-specific information, and Memory Utilization, where it must apply that knowledge to complete tasks with underspecified instructions. The framework focuses on two key personalized knowledge areas: object semantics and user patterns. The authors evaluate several LLM-powered embodied agents using MEMENTO and find that even state-of-the-art models struggle to effectively utilize memory, especially when dealing with multiple memories or tasks involving user patterns. The paper provides an analysis of failure modes and the effect of memory retrieval settings, offering insights for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the design of the MEMENTO framework and the systematic evaluation of memory utilization for *personalized* assistance in embodied agents. While episodic memory and personalization have been explored separately, the specific combination within a controlled embodied agent setting and the focus on personalized knowledge acquisition and retrieval are relatively new. The decomposition into acquisition and utilization stages is valuable for isolating memory-related performance from other factors. The focus on object semantics and user patterns as key personalization axes is also well-motivated and provides a structured approach.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important limitation of current embodied agents, which often rely on single-turn interactions and lack the ability to learn and adapt to individual user preferences. By highlighting the challenges in leveraging memory for personalized assistance, the paper points towards a critical area for future research and development. The failure mode analysis and case studies offer concrete insights into the limitations of current LLMs and inform potential solutions. The framework itself provides a standardized benchmark for evaluating future memory-enhanced embodied agents. The observation about the vulnerability of current LLMs to irrelevant memories also has significance in the broader context of LLM reliability and robustness.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Framework:** MEMENTO provides a clear and structured methodology for evaluating memory utilization.
    *   **Targeted Evaluation:** The focus on personalized knowledge (object semantics and user patterns) allows for a more focused analysis.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper includes a detailed analysis of failure modes, the impact of memory retrieval settings, and the importance of trajectory information.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The findings provide valuable insights for future research directions, such as improved memory filtering techniques and enhanced reasoning over complex sequential information.
    *   Reproducibility via Website: The authors provide access to a website with project information.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simulated Environment:** The experiments are conducted in a simulated environment, which limits the generalizability of the findings to real-world settings. Factors such as perception noise and actuation uncertainty are not considered.
    *   **Oracle Skills:** The use of oracle skills simplifies the embodied agent setup and enables better isolation of the memory component. But results may not translate to less controlled skill selection.
    *   **Limited User Study:** The paper lacks any human user studies to validate the definition of personalization categories (object semantics, user patterns). The task definitions are defined by the authors, making it possible that what they defined to measure memory use is not representative of how humans naturally provide personalized assistance.
    *   **Ambiguous Instructions Experiment (Sec. 6.2):** the improvement by simply having a baseline may show results in Sec. 6.2 can be accounted for by the extra training and not really by an innate ability to resolve indirect knowledge references.
    *   **Dataset size:** While the dataset size is reasonably large, the episodes involving multiple memories are much more limited. Additional testing with more diverse multi-memory examples would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Model Coverage:** While the paper does cover multiple models, there have been continual advancements in LLMs, and more recent iterations could potentially show improved performance.

* **Justification:** MEMENTO addresses a clear gap in the evaluation of embodied agents, and the approach of isolating and evaluating personalized memory is valuable. While it relies on a simulation environment and oracle skills and lacks any user studies, the rigorous methodology and detailed analysis of failure modes contribute meaningful insights. In particular, the focus on personalization (beyond simple instruction-following) in such setups is innovative.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Style Transfer with Diffusion Models for Synthetic-to-Real Domain Adaptation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16360v1)
- **Authors**: Estelle Chigot, Dennis G. Wilson, Meriem Ghrib, Thomas Oberlin
- **Abstract**: Semantic segmentation models trained on synthetic data often perform poorly on real-world images due to domain gaps, particularly in adverse conditions where labeled data is scarce. Yet, recent foundation models enable to generate realistic images without any training. This paper proposes to leverage such diffusion models to improve the performance of vision models when learned on synthetic data. We introduce two novel techniques for semantically consistent style transfer using diffusion models: Class-wise Adaptive Instance Normalization and Cross-Attention (CACTI) and its extension with selective attention Filtering (CACTIF). CACTI applies statistical normalization selectively based on semantic classes, while CACTIF further filters cross-attention maps based on feature similarity, preventing artifacts in regions with weak cross-attention correspondences. Our methods transfer style characteristics while preserving semantic boundaries and structural coherence, unlike approaches that apply global transformations or generate content without constraints. Experiments using GTA5 as source and Cityscapes/ACDC as target domains show that our approach produces higher quality images with lower FID scores and better content preservation. Our work demonstrates that class-aware diffusion-based style transfer effectively bridges the synthetic-to-real domain gap even with minimal target domain data, advancing robust perception systems for challenging real-world applications. The source code is available at: https://github.com/echigot/cactif.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of semantic segmentation models trained on synthetic data performing poorly on real-world images due to domain gaps, particularly in adverse conditions. It proposes two novel techniques leveraging diffusion models for semantically consistent style transfer to bridge this gap: Class-wise Adaptive Instance Normalization and Cross-Attention (CACTI), and its extension with Selective Attention Filtering (CACTIF). CACTI applies statistical normalization selectively based on semantic classes, while CACTIF further filters cross-attention maps based on feature similarity to prevent artifacts. The methods aim to transfer style characteristics while preserving semantic boundaries and structural coherence. Experiments using GTA5 as the source domain and Cityscapes/ACDC as target domains demonstrate that the proposed approach generates higher quality images (lower FID scores) with better content preservation and improves semantic segmentation performance, especially in adverse weather conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces two new techniques (CACTI and CACTIF) that are novel in their specific combination of class-wise adaptive normalization and selective attention filtering within a diffusion-based style transfer framework for domain adaptation. Applying diffusion models to domain adaptation is not entirely new, but the focus on semantic consistency and the specific mechanisms to achieve it represent a contribution.  The filtering of cross-attention based on feature similarity is a particularly noteworthy novel element. However, Adaptive Instance Normalization and cross-attention have individually been used in style transfer before; the paper's novelty lies in their adaptation and combination within the context of diffusion models and domain adaptation.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the demonstrated improvement in semantic segmentation performance, especially in challenging adverse weather conditions. This has practical implications for applications like autonomous driving, where robust perception is crucial. The paper effectively shows that the proposed style transfer methods can generate training data that leads to better performing segmentation models without requiring large amounts of labeled real-world data. The few-shot style transfer aspect is also valuable. However, the paper lacks extensive ablation studies to fully justify the architecture. It would benefit from showing the impact of *each* component in CACTIF to demonstrate the benefits of their joint use.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined and explained methods (CACTI and CACTIF).
    *   Solid experimental results demonstrating improved image quality and segmentation performance.
    *   Focus on a practical and challenging domain adaptation scenario (adverse weather conditions).
    *   Good qualitative results that visually illustrate the benefits of the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited ablation studies: More in-depth ablation studies would be beneficial.
    *   Lack of comparison with state-of-the-art domain adaptation methods that do *not* use diffusion models, limiting the scope of the comparison. It is shown to outperform similar diffusion based approaches.
    *   Limited discussion of computational cost: The paper would benefit from a discussion of the computational cost associated with the proposed methods. This is important for practical applications.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in domain adaptation, particularly in leveraging diffusion models for generating training data. The emphasis on semantic consistency and the proposed CACTI/CACTIF techniques could inspire new approaches for bridging the synthetic-to-real domain gap. However, the relatively incremental nature of the improvement compared to standard DA methods reduces the impact somewhat.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a technically sound approach with demonstrated improvements in a relevant application domain. However, the novelty, while present, is somewhat incremental, and the lack of more extensive ablation studies and limited discussion of computational cost and wider scope comparison holds it back.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A collaborative constrained graph diffusion model for the generation of realistic synthetic molecules
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16365v1)
- **Authors**: Manuel Ruiz-Botella, Marta Sales-Pardo, Roger Guimerà
- **Abstract**: Developing new molecular compounds is crucial to address pressing challenges, from health to environmental sustainability. However, exploring the molecular space to discover new molecules is difficult due to the vastness of the space. Here we introduce CoCoGraph, a collaborative and constrained graph diffusion model capable of generating molecules that are guaranteed to be chemically valid. Thanks to the constraints built into the model and to the collaborative mechanism, CoCoGraph outperforms state-of-the-art approaches on standard benchmarks while requiring up to an order of magnitude fewer parameters. Analysis of 36 chemical properties also demonstrates that CoCoGraph generates molecules with distributions more closely matching real molecules than current models. Leveraging the model's efficiency, we created a database of 8.2M million synthetically generated molecules and conducted a Turing-like test with organic chemistry experts to further assess the plausibility of the generated molecules, and potential biases and limitations of CoCoGraph.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CoCoGraph, a collaborative constrained graph diffusion model designed for generating realistic, chemically valid synthetic molecules. CoCoGraph uses a discrete diffusion process involving double edge swapping, ensuring that atoms maintain their correct valence throughout the process.  A key aspect is a collaborative mechanism employing two models: a diffusion model predicting double edge swaps and a time model estimating the progress of the denoising process. The authors demonstrate that CoCoGraph outperforms state-of-the-art methods on standard benchmarks, generating molecules with property distributions closer to real molecules while requiring significantly fewer parameters.  The paper also presents a large database of synthetically generated molecules and evaluates the plausibility of generated molecules through a Turing-like test with organic chemistry experts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of constrained graph diffusion with a collaborative mechanism is a significant advance. Diffusion models for molecule generation are already an active area, but the explicit constraints to ensure chemical validity *during* the diffusion process rather than relying on post-filtering and the collaboration of two models are quite novel.  The reduction in parameter count is another notable advancement.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in several factors:
    *   **Improved Validity and Realism:** The paper convincingly demonstrates that CoCoGraph generates chemically valid molecules with realistic property distributions, outperforming other methods.  This addresses a major challenge in generative molecular design.
    *   **Efficiency:**  The reduced parameter count translates to greater computational efficiency, making molecule generation more accessible.
    *   **Scalability:** The large database of synthetic molecules generated highlights the potential for CoCoGraph to explore chemical space more efficiently.
    *   **Turing-like Test:** The Turing test provides valuable insight into the plausibility of the generated molecules from the perspective of domain experts, going beyond benchmark metrics. The results indicate that the model generates molecules that are often difficult to distinguish from real ones.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:**  The paper includes comprehensive benchmarking on the GuacaMol benchmark, detailed analysis of various chemical properties, and a Turing-like test.
    *   **Clear Explanation:**  The methods and results are clearly described and well-supported by figures and tables.
    *   **Addresses Key Challenges:** The paper effectively tackles the challenges of chemical validity, diversity, and realism in molecular generation.
    * **Parameter Efficiency**: A major strength is the reduced parameter count, making the model more practical.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Complexity:** While the model is parameter efficient, the O(n4) complexity of double edge swaps is a potential limitation for very large molecules. The paper acknowledges this.
    *  **Dependency on starting molecular formulas:** The model is only able to generate molecules whose formula the user specifies.
    *   **Bias analysis:** While the Turing Test reveals biases in the model, further experiments should explore biases that are more difficult to analyze and that might be problematic.

*   **Potential Influence:** CoCoGraph has the potential to significantly impact the field of molecular design by providing a more efficient and reliable tool for generating novel molecules with desired properties. The database of generated molecules can also serve as a valuable resource for researchers.

**Justification:**

CoCoGraph is a notable advancement in the field of molecular generation due to its enhanced chemical validity, realism, and efficiency. Its approach addresses long-standing challenges and opens up new possibilities for exploring chemical space. However, the computational complexity and potential for certain biases need to be considered.
