- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### ReCopilot: Reverse Engineering Copilot in Binary Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16366v1)
- **Authors**: Guoqiang Chen, Huiqi Sun, Daguang Liu, Zhiqi Wang, Qiang Wang, Bin Yin, Lu Liu, Lingyun Ying
- **Abstract**: Binary analysis plays a pivotal role in security domains such as malware detection and vulnerability discovery, yet it remains labor-intensive and heavily reliant on expert knowledge. General-purpose large language models (LLMs) perform well in programming analysis on source code, while binaryspecific LLMs are underexplored. In this work, we present ReCopilot, an expert LLM designed for binary analysis tasks. ReCopilot integrates binary code knowledge through a meticulously constructed dataset, encompassing continue pretraining (CPT), supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and direct preference optimization (DPO) stages. It leverages variable data flow and call graph to enhance context awareness and employs test-time scaling to improve reasoning capabilities. Evaluations on a comprehensive binary analysis benchmark demonstrate that ReCopilot achieves state-of-the-art performance in tasks such as function name recovery and variable type inference on the decompiled pseudo code, outperforming both existing tools and LLMs by 13%. Our findings highlight the effectiveness of domain-specific training and context enhancement, while also revealing challenges in building super long chain-of-thought. ReCopilot represents a significant step toward automating binary analysis with interpretable and scalable AI assistance in this domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ReCopilot paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ReCopilot, an expert large language model (LLM) specifically designed to assist reverse engineers in binary analysis tasks.  ReCopilot distinguishes itself by integrating binary code knowledge through a carefully curated dataset and a multi-stage training process: continued pretraining (CPT), supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and direct preference optimization (DPO).  The model also leverages static program analysis techniques (variable data flow, call graphs) to enhance its understanding of code context and employs a test-time scaling mechanism to improve reasoning. The authors create a comprehensive benchmark to assess ReCopilot's performance on tasks such as function name recovery and variable type inference, demonstrating state-of-the-art results compared to existing tools and general-purpose LLMs. The demo of ReCopilot is publicly accessible.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the emerging field of applying LLMs to binary analysis.  Here's a breakdown of its strengths and weaknesses:

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Domain-Specific Expertise:**  The paper convincingly argues for the need for domain-specific training for LLMs in binary analysis. The creation of a specialized dataset with CPT, SFT, and DPO is a significant strength, as it directly addresses the limitations of using general-purpose LLMs for this task. The performance improvements validate this approach.
    *   **Context Enhancement:** The integration of static program analysis (call graph, data flow analysis) into the prompt engineering is a valuable contribution.  This allows the LLM to reason about the code in a more informed manner, mimicking how human analysts approach binary analysis.  The paper provides clear descriptions of how this context information is incorporated.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The construction of a multi-task benchmark is a major strength.  It moves beyond single-task evaluations and allows for a more holistic assessment of the model's capabilities in a real-world binary analysis scenario.  The selection of tasks (function name recovery, variable type inference, struct recovery, decompilation, summarization) is relevant and practical.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized.  The methodology is clearly explained, and the results are presented in a clear and concise manner.
    *  **Test-Time Scaling Ability**: The paper implements a generator-discriminator framework for building supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data of binary analysis tasks to support a reasoning model that can take a deep thinking for the user-specific task

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Super-CoT Effectiveness:** The impact of the Super-CoT training is not clearly demonstrated. While the idea is interesting, the ablation study suggests limited improvement, possibly due to dataset imbalance. It creates complexity with limited gains.
    *   **Dataset Composition Details:** While the paper mentions the size of the datasets, further details about the specific types of code and binaries included would be beneficial. For example, what types of open-source projects were used? How much variety in architectures was included? This would allow readers to better assess the generalizability of the model.
    *   **Comparison to Commercial Tools:** While Sidekick (Binary Ninja plugin) is included in the evaluation, the evaluation lacks a thorough discussion of its internal workings, creating potential gaps in comparison.  A more detailed discussion of the commercial tool's strengths and weaknesses compared to ReCopilot would be valuable.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** The paper discusses the "Succ" ratio as a measure of formatting success, but a deeper error analysis would be valuable. What types of errors are most common? Are there specific code patterns that the model struggles with?
    *   **Scope limitation**: ReCopilot focuses primarily on the most fundamental and critical tasks for decompilation augmentation and currently works on decompiled pseudo code only.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The primary novelty lies in the combination of domain-specific training data, context enhancement using static analysis, and a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating binary analysis tasks.  While individual components (e.g., using LLMs for code analysis) have been explored previously, the integration of these elements into a single, well-engineered system is a significant advancement.
    *   The significance stems from the potential to automate and improve the efficiency of binary analysis, a traditionally labor-intensive and expert-dependent process.  ReCopilot could lower the barrier to entry for reverse engineering and accelerate vulnerability discovery.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

ReCopilot represents a significant step forward in applying LLMs to binary analysis. The domain-specific training, context enhancement, and comprehensive benchmark are valuable contributions. The results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing tools, justifying the approach. While the Super-CoT component and the scope limitation have areas of improvement and the paper could benefit from a deeper error analysis and more details on the dataset composition and comparisons to commercial tools, the overall contribution is solid. The paper has the potential to influence future research in this area and to be deployed as a practical tool for security analysts.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Chain-of-Thought Poisoning Attacks against R1-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16367v1)
- **Authors**: Hongru Song, Yu-an Liu, Ruqing Zhang, Jiafeng Guo, Yixing Fan
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems can effectively mitigate the hallucination problem of large language models (LLMs),but they also possess inherent vulnerabilities. Identifying these weaknesses before the large-scale real-world deployment of RAG systems is of great importance, as it lays the foundation for building more secure and robust RAG systems in the future. Existing adversarial attack methods typically exploit knowledge base poisoning to probe the vulnerabilities of RAG systems, which can effectively deceive standard RAG models. However, with the rapid advancement of deep reasoning capabilities in modern LLMs, previous approaches that merely inject incorrect knowledge are inadequate when attacking RAG systems equipped with deep reasoning abilities. Inspired by the deep thinking capabilities of LLMs, this paper extracts reasoning process templates from R1-based RAG systems, uses these templates to wrap erroneous knowledge into adversarial documents, and injects them into the knowledge base to attack RAG systems. The key idea of our approach is that adversarial documents, by simulating the chain-of-thought patterns aligned with the model's training signals, may be misinterpreted by the model as authentic historical reasoning processes, thus increasing their likelihood of being referenced. Experiments conducted on the MS MARCO passage ranking dataset demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Chain-of-Thought Poisoning Attacks against R1-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation Systems" investigates vulnerabilities in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, focusing specifically on systems that utilize large language models (LLMs) with strong reasoning capabilities (R1-based). It argues that existing knowledge base poisoning attacks, which inject incorrect information, are less effective against these more advanced RAG systems. The core idea is to craft adversarial documents that mimic the chain-of-thought reasoning patterns that the R1-based LLMs are trained on. By wrapping erroneous information within these reasoning templates, the attack aims to mislead the LLM into misinterpreting the document as an authentic reasoning process, thereby increasing the likelihood of the incorrect knowledge being used. The authors conduct experiments on the MS MARCO dataset and demonstrate improved attack success rates compared to baseline methods, particularly against R1-based RAG systems.  They also include human evaluations to assess the naturalness of the generated adversarial documents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel attack strategy that goes beyond simple knowledge injection by targeting the reasoning process of advanced RAG systems. The idea of mimicking chain-of-thought patterns to craft adversarial documents is interesting and presents a distinct approach compared to existing knowledge poisoning techniques. The paper addresses a critical vulnerability that may be more prevalent in future RAG systems, as R1-based models become more widespread.

* **Significance:** The research highlights an important security concern in RAG systems, especially as they incorporate more sophisticated LLMs. The ability to manipulate the reasoning process, rather than just injecting faulty knowledge, has significant implications for the trustworthiness and reliability of these systems. The findings suggest that improving the factuality of the retrieved context is not enough; robust methods are also needed to ensure that the LLM's reasoning over the context is not compromised. The paper's discovery of a new vulnerability that is more potent than knowledge poisoning for R1-based RAG has the potential to change the direction of future studies in this domain.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing attacks and provides a strong rationale for the proposed approach.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are well-designed and compare the proposed method against several representative baselines. The inclusion of ASR, ASRr, and ASRg helps to isolate the impact of the attack on both the retriever and generator. Model parameter scaling experiments further provide insights into the robustness trade-offs.
    * **Human Evaluation:** Assessing the naturalness of the adversarial documents through human evaluation is a crucial element for validating the practicality of the attack. The result shows that the method is able to generate plausible adversarial documents.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with a clear explanation of the problem, method, and results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The experiments are primarily focused on the MS MARCO dataset, which may not fully represent the diversity of real-world RAG applications. Further experiments on other datasets are needed.
    * **Reliance on Auxiliary LLM:** The attack relies on an auxiliary LLM to generate adversarial documents.  While the authors aim to use the same base model, the performance of the attack may be affected if a different model is used or if the auxiliary model's capabilities differ.
    * **Incremental improvement on standard models:** The results in Table 2 show that the performance of the current method is not significantly better than other methods on standard RAG system, indicating room for improvement.
    * **Practicality of Chain of Thought Extraction:** The method requires extracting the chain-of-thought reasoning format of the target RAG system. In real world, this reasoning template may not be easily extracted.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research into the security and robustness of RAG systems. The proposed attack method provides a valuable tool for evaluating the vulnerabilities of these systems and developing countermeasures.

**Rigorous Rationale:** The paper has merits in that it presents a new attack method for RAG systems based on chain-of-thought reasoning process that is well-motivated and evaluated. It also offers new perspective on RAG system security that goes beyond knowledge poisoning. However, the method depends on the auxiliary LLM, shows incremental improvement, and does not have a good generalization ability. Given these considerations, I would rate this paper as a good contribution, but not an exceptional one.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### SATURN: SAT-based Reinforcement Learning to Unleash Language Model Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16368v1)
- **Authors**: Huanyu Liu, Jia Li, Hao Zhu, Kechi Zhang, Yihong Dong, Ge Li
- **Abstract**: How to design reinforcement learning (RL) tasks that effectively unleash the reasoning capability of large language models (LLMs) remains an open question. Existing RL tasks (e.g., math, programming, and constructing reasoning tasks) suffer from three key limitations: (1) Scalability. They rely heavily on human annotation or expensive LLM synthesis to generate sufficient training data. (2) Verifiability. LLMs' outputs are hard to verify automatically and reliably. (3) Controllable Difficulty. Most tasks lack fine-grained difficulty control, making it hard to train LLMs to develop reasoning ability from easy to hard. To address these limitations, we propose Saturn, a SAT-based RL framework that uses Boolean Satisfiability (SAT) problems to train and evaluate LLM reasoning. Saturn enables scalable task construction, rule-based verification, and precise difficulty control. Saturn designs a curriculum learning pipeline that continuously improves LLMs' reasoning capability by constructing SAT tasks of increasing difficulty and training LLMs from easy to hard. To ensure stable training, we design a principled mechanism to control difficulty transitions. We introduce Saturn-2.6k, a dataset of 2,660 SAT problems with varying difficulty. It supports the evaluation of how LLM reasoning changes with problem difficulty. We apply Saturn to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen and obtain Saturn-1.5B and Saturn-7B. We achieve several notable results: (1) On SAT problems, Saturn-1.5B and Saturn-7B achieve average pass@3 improvements of +14.0 and +28.1, respectively. (2) On math and programming tasks, Saturn-1.5B and Saturn-7B improve average scores by +4.9 and +1.8 on benchmarks (e.g., AIME, LiveCodeBench). (3) Compared to the state-of-the-art (SOTA) approach in constructing RL tasks, Saturn achieves further improvements of +8.8%. We release the source code, data, and models to support future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SATURN paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SATURN, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that uses Boolean Satisfiability (SAT) problems to train and improve the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs).  The authors identify limitations of existing RL tasks for LLM reasoning, including scalability, verifiability, and controllable difficulty. SATURN addresses these by leveraging SAT's inherent properties: SAT instances can be generated at scale programmatically, solutions are easily verified through rule-based systems, and difficulty can be precisely controlled by adjusting the problem parameters (number of variables, clauses, etc.). The framework incorporates a curriculum learning approach where LLMs are trained on increasingly difficult SAT tasks. The authors introduce SATURN-2.6k, a SAT problem dataset with varying difficulty levels. They then train DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B and 7B models using SATURN, demonstrating improved performance on SAT tasks and transferability to math and programming benchmarks. The results suggest that SATURN effectively enhances LLM reasoning and offers a scalable, verifiable, and controllable training paradigm.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using SAT problems for LLM reasoning training is relatively novel. While prior work has explored SAT as an *evaluation* benchmark, the use of SAT within a *reinforcement learning* framework for *training* LLMs represents a significant departure. The paper also innovates by providing a method for finely controlling the difficulty of the training tasks.
* **Significance:**
    * **Addressing Key Limitations:** The paper directly tackles the scalability, verifiability, and controllable difficulty issues that plague existing RL training methods for LLMs. The ability to generate unlimited training data programmatically, ensure reward correctness through rule-based verification, and control task difficulty for curriculum learning is a major advantage.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results are compelling. The improvements on SAT tasks themselves, the transfer to math and programming, and the comparison with existing RL training methods all demonstrate the effectiveness of the SATURN approach. The creation and release of the SATURN-2.6k dataset also provide a valuable resource for the research community.
    * **Improved Reasoning Patterns:** The analysis of LLM reasoning trajectories showing increased response length and self-verification patterns suggests that SATURN influences the *quality* of reasoning, not just the accuracy of the final answer.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Principled Approach:** The paper is well-motivated and provides a strong theoretical justification for using SAT problems.
    * **Scalability:** SATURN’s ability to generate training data programmatically is a major advantage, especially as LLMs grow in size and require more data.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a variety of experiments and ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of SATURN.
    * **Open Source Contribution:** Releasing the code, data, and models enhances reproducibility and enables further research.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Reasoning:** SATURN primarily focuses on formal logical reasoning, which may not directly translate to all types of reasoning required in more complex, real-world scenarios. This limits the types of knowledge that LLMs can acquire using SATURN.
    * **Overfitting Risk:**  Although the results suggest generalization, there's always a risk of overfitting to the structure of SAT problems. The authors address this by testing on downstream tasks, but further exploration is needed.
    * **Complexity of Implementation:** While the core idea is elegant, the implementation of SATURN, including the difficulty estimation and GRPO training loop, may be complex for some researchers to adopt. The word cloud generated in GPQA does not seem to showcase more "complex" reasoning.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the approach, the significance in addressing limitations of existing RL tasks, the comprehensive experimental results, and the open-source contribution, the paper warrants a high score.  While the potential limitations around the scope of reasoning and the risk of overfitting are valid concerns, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The paper has the potential to significantly influence how LLMs are trained for reasoning.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### PaTH Attention: Position Encoding via Accumulating Householder Transformations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16381v1)
- **Authors**: Songlin Yang, Yikang Shen, Kaiyue Wen, Shawn Tan, Mayank Mishra, Liliang Ren, Rameswar Panda, Yoon Kim
- **Abstract**: The attention mechanism is a core primitive in modern large language models (LLMs) and AI more broadly. Since attention by itself is permutation-invariant, position encoding is essential for modeling structured domains such as language. Rotary position encoding (RoPE) has emerged as the de facto standard approach for position encoding and is part of many modern LLMs. However, in RoPE the key/query transformation between two elements in a sequence is only a function of their relative position and otherwise independent of the actual input. This limits the expressivity of RoPE-based transformers. This paper describes PaTH, a flexible data-dependent position encoding scheme based on accumulated products of Householder(like) transformations, where each transformation is data-dependent, i.e., a function of the input. We derive an efficient parallel algorithm for training through exploiting a compact representation of products of Householder matrices, and implement a FlashAttention-style blockwise algorithm that minimizes I/O cost. Across both targeted synthetic benchmarks and moderate-scale real-world language modeling experiments, we find that PaTH demonstrates superior performance compared to RoPE and other recent baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PaTH Attention: Position Encoding via Accumulating Householder Transformations."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PaTH, a novel position encoding scheme for attention mechanisms in transformers.  PaTH addresses limitations of Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE), the current standard, by making position encoding data-dependent.  It achieves this by accumulating Householder-like transformations along the sequence, where each transformation is a function of the input. The authors derive an efficient parallel training algorithm utilizing a compact representation of Householder matrix products and a FlashAttention-style blockwise algorithm to minimize I/O costs. They demonstrate PaTH's superiority over RoPE and other baselines in synthetic benchmarks and real-world language modeling experiments, especially for tasks requiring state tracking and length extrapolation. They also show that PaTH, when combined with Forgetting Transformer (FoX), achieves further performance improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core idea of data-dependent position encoding is not entirely novel, as previous works like DaPE and variations of ALiBi explore content-dependent bias terms. However, PaTH's unique approach of accumulating Householder transformations is a significant departure from prior methods. The derivation of efficient training algorithms and the combination with FlashAttention contribute additional novel technical aspects. A significant aspect of the novelty lies in adapting techniques from linear RNNs (like DeltaNets) to the transformer setting. The theoretical result showing that PaTH can solve NC¹-complete problems provides further novelty.
* **Significance:**  The paper's potential significance lies in addressing a known limitation of RoPE, specifically its data-independent nature which hinders state tracking and length generalization.  The empirical results on synthetic tasks clearly demonstrate PaTH's advantage in scenarios where RoPE struggles. The improvements on moderate-scale language modeling benchmarks are also promising, suggesting PaTH could become a viable alternative to RoPE in practical applications. The improvement in code modeling is particularly interesting and supports the claim that PaTH aids state-tracking. The fact that PaTH is also compatible with FoX is also a positive.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of RoPE.
    * **Novel Approach:** The Householder transformation-based accumulation is a technically interesting and novel approach to data-dependent positional encoding.
    * **Efficient Implementation:**  The FlashAttention-like implementation is crucial for scaling PaTH to real-world problems and makes it practically relevant.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides compelling empirical evidence of PaTH's superiority, particularly on synthetic tasks, and improved real-world language modeling and length extrapolation.
    * **Theoretical Justification:** The theorem showing that PaTH can solve NC¹-complete problems provides theoretical support for its increased expressiveness.
    * **Compatibility:** The compatibility with other approaches, such as FoX, adds value and makes PaTH a flexible component.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** The increased complexity of PaTH compared to RoPE could be a barrier to adoption. While the authors provide efficient implementations, the computational overhead might be noticeable in some applications.
    * **Limited Scale:** While improvements are shown on 760M parameter models, experiments on much larger models (e.g., 100B+ parameters) would further strengthen the claims and better assess the scalability.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The paper could benefit from more ablation studies to better understand the contribution of different components of PaTH. For example, how much of the performance gain comes from the Householder transformations versus the data-dependent aspect?
    * **Inference Efficiency:** While the paper attempts to maintain inference efficiency (section 3.4), it is less clear if this is actually implemented or how it truly compares with alternative schemes in terms of throughput and latency.

* **Potential Influence:**  PaTH has the potential to influence the design of future attention mechanisms, especially in applications where state tracking and length generalization are critical. If further scaled and optimized, PaTH could become a standard position encoding technique in large language models. The insight from this paper could also spur other research directions related to data-dependent or algebraic position encoding.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and technically sound approach to data-dependent position encoding with solid empirical validation. While not a complete paradigm shift, it represents a significant advancement over RoPE, addressing known limitations with a novel and efficient approach. The theoretical results further bolster the significance of the contribution. The key strengths include the algorithm's novelty, empirical performance, and the authors' theoretical analysis of its representational capacity. Weaknesses include the increased complexity relative to existing methods and a limited evaluation on larger models and efficiency comparisons. Overall, PaTH represents a worthwhile contribution to the field with significant potential impact.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Semantic Pivots Enable Cross-Lingual Transfer in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16385v1)
- **Authors**: Kaiyu He, Tong Zhou, Yubo Chen, Delai Qiu, Shengping Liu, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate remarkable ability in cross-lingual tasks. Understanding how LLMs acquire this ability is crucial for their interpretability. To quantify the cross-lingual ability of LLMs accurately, we propose a Word-Level Cross-Lingual Translation Task. To find how LLMs learn cross-lingual ability, we trace the outputs of LLMs' intermediate layers in the word translation task. We identify and distinguish two distinct behaviors in the forward pass of LLMs: co-occurrence behavior and semantic pivot behavior. We attribute LLMs' two distinct behaviors to the co-occurrence frequency of words and find the semantic pivot from the pre-training dataset. Finally, to apply our findings to improve the cross-lingual ability of LLMs, we reconstruct a semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset using documents with a high proportion of semantic pivots. Our experiments validate the effectiveness of our approach in enhancing cross-lingual ability. Our research contributes insights into the interpretability of LLMs and offers a method for improving LLMs' cross-lingual ability.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Semantic Pivots Enable Cross-Lingual Transfer in Large Language Models".

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve cross-lingual transfer capabilities.  The authors propose a Cross-Lingual Word Translation Dataset (CLWTD) and a corresponding metric to quantify this ability at the word level, arguing that sentence-level metrics are too coarse, especially for early checkpoints. They analyze the intermediate layer outputs of LLMs during word translation, identifying two distinct behaviors: co-occurrence behavior and semantic pivot behavior. They attribute these behaviors to the frequency with which words co-occur in the pre-training data and identify semantic pivots used during translation. Finally, the authors construct a semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset by selecting documents with a high proportion of these pivots, showing that this reconstruction improves the cross-lingual ability of an LLM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the need to better understand and quantify cross-lingual ability in LLMs, particularly beyond just machine translation. The motivation for a word-level evaluation is well-argued.
*   **Novelty in Approach:** The analysis of intermediate layers using a "logit lens" to uncover co-occurrence and semantic pivot behaviors represents a valuable contribution. Linking these behaviors to the statistical properties of the pre-training data (co-occurrence frequencies) offers some insight into the mechanisms at play. The development of the semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset is also a worthwhile attempt to leverage the findings.
*   **CLWTD Dataset:** The introduction of CLWTD is a plus, offering a more granular evaluation of cross-lingual word translation abilities, useful in the context of early checkpoints, and the method to create the dataset from available dictionaries and translation engines.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset, showing improvements in cross-lingual ability over baseline approaches on a tested LLM.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** A key limitation is the evaluation on only a single, relatively small LLM (OLMO-1B).  While the authors acknowledge this as a limitation in the paper due to the limited availability of open-source large language models with checkpoints and pre-training data. This makes it difficult to generalize the findings and assess whether the discovered behaviors and the benefits of the semantic pivot-aware dataset hold across different architectures, scales, or pre-training datasets.

*   **Reliance on DeepSeek for Pivot Filtering:**  The method for filtering candidate semantic pivots using DeepSeek introduces a potential bias. The quality and characteristics of DeepSeek's internal knowledge could influence the selected pivots, and this dependence is not sufficiently explored. The prompted Deepseek to filter meaningless token is prone to producing different pivots when input different prompt.

*   **Co-occurrence vs. Semantics:** While the paper distinguishes between co-occurrence and semantic pivot behaviors, there is a potential overlap. High co-occurrence could *be* indicative of a semantic relationship, even if not a direct translation. The study could have benefited from a more nuanced exploration of the semantic relationships between words that co-occur frequently.
*   **Significance of Improvement:** The improvements in cross-lingual ability, while statistically significant, are relatively modest (0.013 improvement). The practical impact of these improvements in downstream applications is not explored. The paper would be stronger if it connected these small improvements to real-world tasks.
*   **Lack of Generalization:** The process of identifying "semantic pivots" and creating a specialized pre-training dataset seems somewhat brittle and potentially difficult to generalize to other language pairs or domains without significant manual effort.

*   **Clarity of "Semantic Pivot":** the term "semantic pivot" is used but the definition of it could have been further clarified, especially in the context of LLMs. It is also unclear what factors contribute to a token being an effective semantic pivot.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's main contribution is the analysis of LLM internals to identify co-occurrence and semantic pivot behaviors and to link them to pre-training data.  The construction of the semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset is also a useful, if not groundbreaking, contribution. The limitations regarding the scope of evaluation and the generalizability of the findings temper the significance of the work. The word-level evaluation approach offers some new insights, although this is built on existing methods such as logit lens.

**Justification for Score:**

The score reflects the paper's interesting insights into LLM behavior and its innovative approach to leveraging those insights for pre-training, but also acknowledges its limited scope and potential for bias. The work contributes to the understanding of LLM interpretability and offers a potential pathway for improving cross-lingual ability, but it requires further validation and generalization before its full impact can be realized.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Resource for Error Analysis in Text Simplification: New Taxonomy and Test Collection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16392v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Vendeville, Liana Ermakova, Pierre De Loor
- **Abstract**: The general public often encounters complex texts but does not have the time or expertise to fully understand them, leading to the spread of misinformation. Automatic Text Simplification (ATS) helps make information more accessible, but its evaluation methods have not kept up with advances in text generation, especially with Large Language Models (LLMs). In particular, recent studies have shown that current ATS metrics do not correlate with the presence of errors. Manual inspections have further revealed a variety of errors, underscoring the need for a more nuanced evaluation framework, which is currently lacking. This resource paper addresses this gap by introducing a test collection for detecting and classifying errors in simplified texts. First, we propose a taxonomy of errors, with a formal focus on information distortion. Next, we introduce a parallel dataset of automatically simplified scientific texts. This dataset has been human-annotated with labels based on our proposed taxonomy. Finally, we analyze the quality of the dataset, and we study the performance of existing models to detect and classify errors from that taxonomy. These contributions give researchers the tools to better evaluate errors in ATS, develop more reliable models, and ultimately improve the quality of automatically simplified texts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the lack of comprehensive error analysis resources for Automatic Text Simplification (ATS). It introduces a novel taxonomy of errors in ATS, particularly focusing on information distortion. The authors then create a parallel dataset of automatically simplified scientific texts, annotating it according to the proposed taxonomy. Finally, they analyze the quality of their dataset and evaluate the performance of existing error detection models on it. This work aims to provide researchers with better tools for evaluating errors in ATS, developing more reliable models, and ultimately improving the quality of simplified texts. The paper also proposes to use the test collection for the SimpleText shared task at CLEF 2025.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by providing a structured taxonomy of errors in ATS.  Existing work often focuses on general text generation errors (fluency, truthfulness) or edit-level errors, while this work explicitly targets errors specific to simplification, with a focus on information distortion (e.g., overgeneralization, loss of informative content). The creation and annotation of a dataset based on this taxonomy is also a valuable contribution. It enables quantitative analysis of different types of simplification errors and provides a benchmark for evaluating error detection methods. The emphasis on a formal definition using set theory is also quite unique. However, edit-level error categorization has been covered by [9] previously.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in ATS research. Current evaluation metrics for ATS often fail to correlate with human judgments of simplification quality, partly because they don't adequately capture the range of errors that can occur. This work helps to remedy that by providing a more granular and nuanced framework for error analysis. The availability of the annotated dataset will facilitate the development of more sophisticated error detection models, which could lead to improvements in ATS systems. The proposed use for CLEF 2025 also increases the impact of this paper.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proposed taxonomy is well-structured and comprehensive.
    *   The annotation scheme appears well-defined, although the inter-annotator agreement raises some concerns (see below).
    *   The empirical evaluation of existing error detection models highlights the limitations of current approaches and points to areas for future research.
    *   The release of the annotated dataset and annotation scheme fosters reproducibility and further research in the field.
    *   The formal definition of the errors is novel and well thought-out.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Inter-Annotator Agreement:** The inter-annotator agreement is a significant concern. While the authors acknowledge this, the relatively low Kappa scores for many error classes (especially Information and Simplification errors) indicate that the annotation scheme may be subjective or ambiguous. This could limit the reliability of the annotated dataset. The decision to keep the annotator with the low consistency (0.56) weakens confidence, though the authors’ note that users can discard those annotations if they prefer. Further refinement of the annotation guidelines and more rigorous training of annotators may be necessary.
    *   **Limited Error Types Studied in Showcase:** While the taxonomy is extensive, the empirical evaluation focuses primarily on hallucination detection models. It would have been beneficial to evaluate a wider range of error detection approaches. The discussion of other error detection approaches is somewhat limited.
    *   **Dataset Size:** While the dataset is a valuable contribution, its size (2,659 annotations) is relatively small, which means the AUPRC calculations are limited.
    *   **Subjectivity in Importance:** The paper acknowledges the subjectivity in defining "important information" for simplification errors, which introduces some ambiguity into the evaluation. A more detailed discussion of how to address this subjectivity in practice would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the direction of ATS research by promoting a more error-aware approach to evaluation and model development. The taxonomy and dataset will likely be used as a benchmark by other researchers in the field.

**Score:** 7/10

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable and novel contribution to the field of ATS by introducing a structured taxonomy of simplification errors and providing an annotated dataset based on this taxonomy. It addresses a clear gap in current ATS research and has the potential to influence future work by promoting more error-aware evaluation and model development.  The strong points are the explicit formalization of errors and the creation of the corpus. However, the lower-than-ideal inter-annotator agreement and the limited scope of the empirical evaluation (focusing primarily on hallucination detection) reduce the score. The relatively small dataset size also constrains the generalizability of the findings. However, it's very likely that the work will be useful as a shared task dataset. The score of 7 reflects the significance of the contribution balanced against its limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Divide-Fuse-Conquer: Eliciting "Aha Moments" in Multi-Scenario Games
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16401v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoqing Zhang, Huabin Zheng, Ang Lv, Yuhan Liu, Zirui Song, Flood Sung, Xiuying Chen, Rui Yan
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have been observed to suddenly exhibit advanced reasoning abilities during reinforcement learning (RL), resembling an ``aha moment'' triggered by simple outcome-based rewards. While RL has proven effective in eliciting such breakthroughs in tasks involving mathematics, coding, and vision, it faces significant challenges in multi-scenario games. The diversity of game rules, interaction modes, and environmental complexities often leads to policies that perform well in one scenario but fail to generalize to others. Simply combining multiple scenarios during training introduces additional challenges, such as training instability and poor performance. To overcome these challenges, we propose Divide-Fuse-Conquer, a framework designed to enhance generalization in multi-scenario RL. This approach starts by heuristically grouping games based on characteristics such as rules and difficulties. Specialized models are then trained for each group to excel at games in the group is what we refer to as the divide step. Next, we fuse model parameters from different groups as a new model, and continue training it for multiple groups, until the scenarios in all groups are conquered. Experiments across 18 TextArena games show that Qwen2.5-32B-Align trained with the Divide-Fuse-Conquer strategy reaches a performance level comparable to Claude3.5, achieving 7 wins and 4 draws. We hope our approach can inspire future research on using reinforcement learning to improve the generalization of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Divide-Fuse-Conquer" (DFC), a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve the generalization ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) in multi-scenario game environments. DFC addresses challenges like training instability and poor generalization often encountered when training LLMs with RL across diverse games. The framework consists of three main steps: (1) **Divide:** Heuristically grouping games based on characteristics like rules and difficulty; (2) **Fuse:** Merging model parameters from specialized models trained on individual groups; and (3) **Conquer:** Refine the training process to consolidate knowledge from different game groups effectively. The authors also introduce a suite of techniques to enhance training stability, efficiency, and performance, including format reward shaping, half-negative sampling, epsilon-greedy disturbance, randomized seed initialization, mixed priority sampling, and a hasty-action penalty reward. Experiments across 18 TextArena games demonstrate that a Qwen2.5-32B-Align model trained with DFC achieves performance comparable to Claude3.5.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dividing the problem space and then fusing solutions is not entirely new (it is inspired by the well-known algorithm design paradigm). However, its application to LLM reinforcement learning in multi-scenario environments, along with the specifically tailored techniques for stability, efficiency, and performance improvement, contributes a degree of novelty. The heuristic grouping of games and the specific reward shaping components feel somewhat ad-hoc, but the framework offers a structured approach for a complex problem.

*   **Significance:** Generalization remains a crucial challenge for LLMs, and the ability to train them effectively across diverse environments is highly valuable. The DFC framework provides a practical approach to tackle this issue, offering insights into multi-scenario RL for LLMs. The experimental results, demonstrating competitive performance against a strong baseline (Claude3.5), supports the effectiveness of the approach. However, the limitations of the base model used (Qwen2.5) compared to state-of-the-art LLMs is a weakness. The approach's simplicity is both a strength and a weakness; the heuristics can be expanded upon and researched more.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a real and important problem in LLM RL.
    *   Presents a clear and well-structured framework (DFC).
    *   Offers practical techniques for improving training stability, efficiency, and performance.
    *   Demonstrates competitive results against a strong baseline in a challenging domain.
    *   Analyzes model behaviors and offers valuable insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The game-specific techniques and grouping heuristics may lack strong theoretical grounding.
    *   The model used for experimentation (Qwen2.5-32B) is not the current state-of-the-art LLM, limiting the generalizability of the findings to very large models.
    *   Limited analysis of the scalability of the approach to a larger number of more diverse environments.
    *   Limited novelty as it builds on the "divide and conquer" paradigm.

*   **Potential Impact:** The DFC framework could inspire further research on structured RL strategies for LLMs, particularly in improving generalization and stability.  The techniques introduced to enhance training could be valuable in other multi-task or multi-environment RL settings.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper offers valuable insights and achieves promising results, its novelty is somewhat limited by the well-established "divide and conquer" paradigm. The results are good but constrained by the LLM it used as a baseline. The paper still contributes valuable findings through its clear framework, practical techniques, and empirical validation.  The techniques that it uses are a bit ad-hoc but provide a clear set of experiments that contribute value to the field.

- **Score**: 6/10

### From Surveys to Narratives: Rethinking Cultural Value Adaptation in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16408v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Farid Adilazuarda, Chen Cecilia Liu, Iryna Gurevych, Alham Fikri Aji
- **Abstract**: Adapting cultural values in Large Language Models (LLMs) presents significant challenges, particularly due to biases and limited training data. Prior work primarily aligns LLMs with different cultural values using World Values Survey (WVS) data. However, it remains unclear whether this approach effectively captures cultural nuances or produces distinct cultural representations for various downstream tasks. In this paper, we systematically investigate WVS-based training for cultural value adaptation and find that relying solely on survey data can homogenize cultural norms and interfere with factual knowledge. To investigate these issues, we augment WVS with encyclopedic and scenario-based cultural narratives from Wikipedia and NormAd. While these narratives may have variable effects on downstream tasks, they consistently improve cultural distinctiveness than survey data alone. Our work highlights the inherent complexity of aligning cultural values with the goal of guiding task-specific behavior.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the challenge of adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to reflect different cultural values, specifically moving beyond the Western-centric biases they often exhibit. The authors investigate using the World Values Survey (WVS) data for cultural value adaptation, comparing it to enriching the training data with cultural narratives from Wikipedia and a scenario-based norms dataset (NormAd). The study finds that relying solely on WVS data can lead to a homogenization of cultural norms and potentially interfere with the LLM's factual knowledge.  However, they find that augmenting WVS with narrative data, particularly from NormAd, can improve cultural distinctiveness, although the impact on downstream task performance varies depending on the specific task and model. The paper highlights the complexity of aligning cultural values with task-specific behaviors and advocates for further research into effective data combinations for cultural adaptation.

**Rigorous Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:**  Cultural bias in LLMs is a significant issue that impacts fairness, accessibility, and the global applicability of these models. The paper tackles this directly.
    *   **Systematic Investigation:** The authors conduct a systematic evaluation, comparing different training data strategies and assessing their impact on multiple metrics: cultural distinctiveness, downstream task performance (offensiveness classification), and factual knowledge retention.
    *   **Insightful Findings:**  The findings regarding the limitations of WVS data and the benefits of incorporating narrative data are valuable and contribute to a better understanding of the complexities of cultural adaptation. The observation that survey data alone can *homogenize* cultural understanding is a key takeaway.
    *   **Use of Novel Metrics:**  The introduction of the C-DIST score attempts to quantify cultural distinctiveness in a novel way, showing a potential avenue to evaluate cultural representations in LLMs.
    *   **Use of multiple LLM backbone models:** The study explores three different backbones of LLMs which demonstrates its generalizability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of "Culture":** The paper focuses on linguistic-regional cultures and acknowledges that culture is more nuanced and intersectional. It would be stronger if it addressed limitations of framing culture in this way, including potential dangers of reinforcing monolithic notions of cultural groups.
    *   **Subjectivity in Downstream Tasks:**  While the study uses a quantitative metric (F1 score) for offensiveness classification, offensiveness is inherently subjective and culturally dependent. The choice of datasets for this task might introduce its own biases.
    *   **Unclear Utility of Cultural Adaptation** The study evaluates whether LLMs are culturally distinct but lacks to clearly justify whether such distinctiveness is beneficial for downstream tasks other than for fairness.

    *   **C-DIST metric could be improved** The C-DIST metric is useful, but relies on the potentially problematic assumptions that the models should always perform best on data from their adapted culture and that model's adaptations should always be beneficial to downstream tasks.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper's novelty lies in its systematic comparison of different data sources for cultural value adaptation and its highlighting of the limitations of using survey data alone. While other works have used WVS, this paper critically examines its effectiveness and proposes alternative strategies. The significance is moderate, as it moves beyond simply using existing techniques and contributes to a deeper understanding of the problem.  However, the field of LLM alignment, including cultural alignment, is rapidly evolving, so its long-term impact may be limited without further work building on these findings.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    The paper could influence future research by encouraging a more nuanced approach to cultural value adaptation, considering the need for narrative context and the potential for unintended consequences. It provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating different cultural adaptation techniques and highlights the need for more robust evaluation metrics. It may also spur the development of new datasets and methods for capturing cultural nuances.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** because the paper tackles an important problem, conducts a systematic investigation, and produces insightful findings. However, limitations in the scope of "culture," the reliance on subjective downstream tasks, and the novelty of the metric prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. It's a solid piece of work that advances our understanding of the challenges and opportunities in cultural value adaptation of LLMs, but there is room for improvement in the generalizability and novelty of the method presented.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tool-Star: Empowering LLM-Brained Multi-Tool Reasoner via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16410v1)
- **Authors**: Guanting Dong, Yifei Chen, Xiaoxi Li, Jiajie Jin, Hongjin Qian, Yutao Zhu, Hangyu Mao, Guorui Zhou, Zhicheng Dou, Ji-Rong Wen
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable reasoning capabilities via large-scale reinforcement learning (RL). However, leveraging the RL algorithm to empower effective multi-tool collaborative reasoning in LLMs remains an open challenge. In this paper, we introduce Tool-Star, an RL-based framework designed to empower LLMs to autonomously invoke multiple external tools during stepwise reasoning. Tool-Star integrates six types of tools and incorporates systematic designs in both data synthesis and training. To address the scarcity of tool-use data, we propose a general tool-integrated reasoning data synthesis pipeline, which combines tool-integrated prompting with hint-based sampling to automatically and scalably generate tool-use trajectories. A subsequent quality normalization and difficulty-aware classification process filters out low-quality samples and organizes the dataset from easy to hard. Furthermore, we propose a two-stage training framework to enhance multi-tool collaborative reasoning by: (1) cold-start fine-tuning, which guides LLMs to explore reasoning patterns via tool-invocation feedback; and (2) a multi-tool self-critic RL algorithm with hierarchical reward design, which reinforces reward understanding and promotes effective tool collaboration. Experimental analyses on over 10 challenging reasoning benchmarks highlight the effectiveness and efficiency of Tool-Star. The code is available at https://github.com/dongguanting/Tool-Star.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Tool-Star: Empowering LLM-Brained Multi-Tool Reasoner via Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Tool-Star, a reinforcement learning (RL) based framework designed to enable Large Language Models (LLMs) to autonomously invoke multiple external tools during stepwise reasoning. It addresses the open challenge of empowering LLMs for effective multi-tool collaborative reasoning. Tool-Star includes: a general tool-integrated reasoning data synthesis pipeline combining tool-integrated prompting with hint-based sampling, and a two-stage training framework for multi-tool collaboration (Cold-Start Supervised Fine-Tuning, Multi-Tool Self-Critic Reinforcement Learning). Experimental results on over 10 challenging reasoning benchmarks demonstrate Tool-Star's effectiveness and efficiency. The code is available on GitHub.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the systematic approach to multi-tool collaborative reasoning within an LLM, empowered by RL. While previous work has explored tool use in LLMs, this paper specifically focuses on *collaborative* use, meaning intelligently integrating feedback from multiple tools (search, code interpreter, etc.). The proposed data synthesis pipeline and two-stage training framework are also novel contributions. The framework of using Cold-Start SFT and Self-Critic RL is also a key contribution.
*   **Significance:** The significance is that Tool-Star attempts to address a practical problem hindering wider LLM deployment: enabling LLMs to solve real-world reasoning tasks that demand complex tool integrations. It introduces a system that can scalably and efficiently learn how to use those tools.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** Tool-Star proposes a complete framework, from data generation to training, that aims to improve LLM tool use capabilities systematically.
    *   **Data Synthesis Pipeline:** The tool-integrated reasoning data synthesis pipeline addresses the data scarcity bottleneck.
    *   **Two-Stage Training:** The two-stage training approach guides LLMs from basic tool understanding to more complex, collaborative usage.
    *   **Self-Critic RL:** Hierarchical rewards and self-critic fine-tuning phase are designed to address LLM understanding of complex reward structure.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Thorough experimental validation across a variety of challenging benchmarks provides strong evidence of effectiveness.
    *   **Multi-Tool Focus:** The emphasis on multi-tool collaboration, rather than single-tool usage, reflects a more realistic reasoning setup.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The framework, with its data synthesis and two-stage training, is quite complex. Replicating and adapting it might require considerable effort.
    *   **Benchmark Dependence:** The performance is benchmark-specific, so generalization to completely new, unseen tasks is not fully guaranteed. The success also hinges upon a carefully chosen toolset.
    *   **Computational Cost:** RL training, even with optimizations, is computationally expensive. The paper could have provided more details on resource usage and training times.
    *   **Limited Generalization Guarantees:** The paper primarily demonstrates empirical performance. The theoretical analysis or guarantees about the convergence or optimality of the RL algorithm is not covered.
    *   **Still relies on LLM reasoning:** ToolStar leverages various training schemes, but ultimately its success hinges on the inherent reasoning capabilities of the underlying LLM.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by providing a practical and effective framework for LLM tool use, driving further research into collaborative reasoning and autonomous agent development. It lays the groundwork for more robust and capable LLM-based solutions.

**Score: 8.0**

**Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM research by addressing the important problem of multi-tool collaborative reasoning. The Tool-Star framework and the accompanying data synthesis and training methodologies are innovative and empirically effective. While the complexity of the framework and potential for benchmark dependence are weaknesses, the overall impact of the work is positive, making it a strong contribution that warrants significant attention. It provides a solid foundation for future research to build upon.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Attributing Response to Context: A Jensen-Shannon Divergence Driven Mechanistic Study of Context Attribution in Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16415v1)
- **Authors**: Ruizhe Li, Chen Chen, Yuchen Hu, Yanjun Gao, Xi Wang, Emine Yilmaz
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) leverages large language models (LLMs) combined with external contexts to enhance the accuracy and reliability of generated responses. However, reliably attributing generated content to specific context segments, context attribution, remains challenging due to the computationally intensive nature of current methods, which often require extensive fine-tuning or human annotation. In this work, we introduce a novel Jensen-Shannon Divergence driven method to Attribute Response to Context (ARC-JSD), enabling efficient and accurate identification of essential context sentences without additional fine-tuning or surrogate modelling. Evaluations on a wide range of RAG benchmarks, such as TyDi QA, Hotpot QA, and Musique, using instruction-tuned LLMs in different scales demonstrate superior accuracy and significant computational efficiency improvements compared to the previous surrogate-based method. Furthermore, our mechanistic analysis reveals specific attention heads and multilayer perceptron (MLP) layers responsible for context attribution, providing valuable insights into the internal workings of RAG models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ARC-JSD, a novel and efficient method for attributing generated responses in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems to specific context segments.  ARC-JSD leverages Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) to measure the difference in response distributions generated with the full context versus context-ablated versions. By ranking context sentences based on their JSD scores, the method identifies essential context without requiring computationally expensive fine-tuning or surrogate modeling.  The authors evaluate ARC-JSD across multiple question-answering benchmarks (TyDi QA, Hotpot QA, MuSiQue) using various instruction-tuned LLMs and demonstrate superior accuracy and significant computational efficiency compared to previous methods.  Furthermore, they conduct a mechanistic analysis to pinpoint specific attention heads and multilayer perceptron (MLP) layers responsible for context attribution within RAG LLMs, providing valuable insights into the internal workings of these models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of JSD for context attribution in RAG models. While JSD is a well-established metric for comparing probability distributions, its direct use in this context, bypassing the need for surrogate models or extensive fine-tuning, is a fresh approach. The mechanistic analysis, while building on existing interpretability techniques (Logit Lens), contributes to a deeper understanding of how context is processed within RAG models, providing specific identification of attention heads and MLP layers crucial for attribution. The framework is novel in that it directly applies Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) to quantify the difference in response distributions generated with and without specific context sentences. This direct approach provides a more accurate measure of the importance of context sentences, as it does not rely on the limitations of surrogate models or the potential for non-linearities.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the increased efficiency and improved accuracy in context attribution.  Existing methods often rely on either human annotation (expensive) or computationally intensive techniques like fine-tuning or gradient-based attribution. ARC-JSD provides a more practical solution, especially for large-scale RAG deployments. Improving context attribution enhances the trustworthiness of RAG systems by allowing users to verify the grounding of generated responses, thereby mitigating hallucinations and promoting more reliable outputs.  The mechanistic analysis is valuable for researchers seeking to understand and potentially improve the design of RAG models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Computational Efficiency:**  The paper highlights a key advantage: ARC-JSD's reduced computational cost compared to surrogate-based methods. The method only requires 'n' forward calls of the underlying RAG model, where 'n' is the number of sentences in the prompt, whereas the surrogate based method requires substantially more forward calls of the RAG model, in addition to the time required to train the surrogate model. This efficiency is a major practical benefit.
    *   **Accuracy:** The empirical results demonstrate superior accuracy in context attribution across various datasets and LLMs.
    *   **Interpretability:** The integration with Logit Lens provides a valuable mechanistic understanding of context attribution within RAG models.
    *   **Modularity:** ARC-JSD integrates modularly into existing RAG pipelines.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The method is evaluated with several different LLMs of different parameter scale, including Qwen2 and Gemma, as well as 3 different QA datasets, which provides breadth to the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Assumptions:** The method relies on the assumption that removing crucial context segments significantly impacts the probability distribution of the generated response. While generally valid, this assumption might not hold in all cases, especially if the model can infer or retrieve similar information from other sources or if the query context is highly redundant.
    *   **Computational cost:** While the method does improve on current baselines, there are still many forward calls of the underlying LLM required.
    *   **Generalization:** While the model has been tested on various benchmarks, testing on tasks that involve more complex and nuanced reasoning may be warranted.
    *   **Reliance on Sentence Boundaries:** The ablation is done at the sentence level. There may be cases where portions of sentences are more important than the entire sentence. There may be performance boosts from exploring sub-sentence ablations.

*   **Potential Influence:** ARC-JSD has the potential to influence the design and deployment of RAG systems. Its efficiency and accuracy make it a practical tool for improving the trustworthiness of these models. The mechanistic insights can guide future research on designing more effective context integration mechanisms. It offers a pathway towards more transparent and reliable RAG applications.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the paper presents a solid contribution, its impact is somewhat limited by its reliance on a core assumption and the incremental nature of its improvements on existing methods. The novelty is significant in terms of the specific application of JSD, but the underlying concept of context ablation isn't entirely new. The gains in efficiency and accuracy are valuable, but not transformative. The paper provides a step forward in understanding and improving RAG models, but is short of a breakthrough.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Circle-RoPE: Cone-like Decoupled Rotary Positional Embedding for Large Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16416v1)
- **Authors**: Chengcheng Wang, Jianyuan Guo, Hongguang Li, Yuchuan Tian, Ying Nie, Chang Xu, Kai Han
- **Abstract**: Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) is a widely adopted technique for encoding relative positional information in large language models (LLMs). However, when extended to large vision-language models (LVLMs), its variants introduce unintended cross-modal positional biases. Specifically, they enforce relative positional dependencies between text token indices and image tokens, causing spurious alignments. This issue arises because image tokens representing the same content but located at different spatial positions are assigned distinct positional biases, leading to inconsistent cross-modal associations. To address this, we propose Per-Token Distance (PTD) - a simple yet effective metric for quantifying the independence of positional encodings across modalities. Informed by this analysis, we introduce Circle-RoPE, a novel encoding scheme that maps image token indices onto a circular trajectory orthogonal to the linear path of text token indices, forming a cone-like structure. This configuration ensures that each text token maintains an equal distance to all image tokens, reducing artificial cross-modal biases while preserving intra-image spatial information. To further enhance performance, we propose a staggered layer strategy that applies different RoPE variants across layers. This design leverages the complementary strengths of each RoPE variant, thereby enhancing the model's overall performance. Our experimental results demonstrate that our method effectively preserves spatial information from images while reducing relative positional bias, offering a more robust and flexible positional encoding framework for LVLMs. The code is available at [https://github.com/lose4578/CircleRoPE](https://github.com/lose4578/CircleRoPE).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Circle-RoPE," a novel positional encoding method for Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) designed to address the issue of unintended cross-modal positional biases that arise when standard Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) is directly applied to LVLMs. The core problem identified is that flattening image tokens and concatenating them with text tokens can lead to spurious alignments between text and image regions, even if semantically unrelated. Circle-RoPE tackles this by mapping image token indices onto a circular trajectory orthogonal to the linear path of text token indices, forming a cone-like structure. This ensures that each text token maintains an equal distance to all image tokens, reducing cross-modal bias while preserving intra-image spatial information.  The paper also proposes a Dual-Frame Fusion (DFF) mechanism to enhance intra-image spatial consistency and an Alternating Geometric Encoding (AGE) strategy that switches between different RoPE variants across layers to leverage their complementary strengths.  Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method in preserving spatial information and reducing relative positional bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a conceptually novel approach to positional encoding in LVLMs. While building upon existing RoPE techniques like M-RoPE, the key idea of mapping image tokens to a circle orthogonal to the text token's linear dimension is a significant departure and directly addresses a specific identified weakness of prior methods. The Dual-Frame Fusion and Alternating Geometric Encoding are also novel refinements that contribute to the overall system.

*   **Significance:** The problem of cross-modal positional bias is a significant concern in LVLMs, as it directly impacts the quality of multimodal understanding and reasoning.  Circle-RoPE offers a principled solution to this problem, which could lead to improved performance in various vision-language tasks.  The paper supports its claims with experimental results on various challenging benchmarks.  The improvement, while seemingly modest, demonstrates a solid advantage over the baseline approach.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of cross-modal positional bias and provides a metric (PTD) to quantify it.
    *   **Well-Motivated Solution:** The Circle-RoPE approach is well-motivated and directly addresses the identified limitations of previous methods.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a range of experiments to validate the effectiveness of the proposed method and analyze the contributions of different components.
    *   **Complete Ablation Study:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of each component of the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results show improvement, they aren't dramatically better than the baseline. This suggests the technique might be a useful refinement, not a paradigm shift.
    *   **Complexity:** The Circle-RoPE encoding introduces additional complexity compared to simpler RoPE variants. This could make it more computationally expensive to train and deploy.
    *   **Scope:** Focuses mostly on positional embedding. The approach itself does not introduce new architecture or training strategies of LVLM.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to influence the design of positional encoding schemes in future LVLMs. The approach may also be useful for other multimodal tasks where it is important to decouple the spatial relationships of different modalities. The metric PTD is an excellent contribution that might be adopted by other researchers when designing future multimodal models.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

Given the novelty of the approach and its potential to address a significant problem in LVLMs, but noting the limitations in achieved performance lift over the baseline as well as additional complexity, I would assign a score of **7**. The work presents a well-motivated and potentially influential technique with reasonable empirical validation, making it a good contribution to the field, even if not a ground-breaking one.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### WebAgent-R1: Training Web Agents via End-to-End Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16421v1)
- **Authors**: Zhepei Wei, Wenlin Yao, Yao Liu, Weizhi Zhang, Qin Lu, Liang Qiu, Changlong Yu, Puyang Xu, Chao Zhang, Bing Yin, Hyokun Yun, Lihong Li
- **Abstract**: While reinforcement learning (RL) has demonstrated remarkable success in enhancing large language models (LLMs), it has primarily focused on single-turn tasks such as solving math problems. Training effective web agents for multi-turn interactions remains challenging due to the complexity of long-horizon decision-making across dynamic web interfaces. In this work, we present WebAgent-R1, a simple yet effective end-to-end multi-turn RL framework for training web agents. It learns directly from online interactions with web environments by asynchronously generating diverse trajectories, entirely guided by binary rewards depending on task success. Experiments on the WebArena-Lite benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of WebAgent-R1, boosting the task success rate of Qwen-2.5-3B from 6.1% to 33.9% and Llama-3.1-8B from 8.5% to 44.8%, significantly outperforming existing state-of-the-art methods and strong proprietary models such as OpenAI o3. In-depth analyses reveal the effectiveness of the thinking-based prompting strategy and test-time scaling through increased interactions for web tasks. We further investigate different RL initialization policies by introducing two variants, namely WebAgent-R1-Zero and WebAgent-R1-CoT, which highlight the importance of the warm-up training stage (i.e., behavior cloning) and provide insights on incorporating long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in web agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the WEBAGENT-R1 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WEBAGENT-R1, a framework for training web agents using end-to-end multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL). It focuses on addressing the challenges of long-horizon decision-making in dynamic web environments. WEBAGENT-R1 utilizes a dynamic context compression mechanism to manage memory overhead and asynchronous trajectory rollouts for efficient training.  The method extends Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to multi-turn scenarios (M-GRPO). The agents are trained directly through online interactions, guided by binary success/failure rewards. Experiments on the WebArena-Lite benchmark demonstrate significant performance improvements compared to existing methods, including prompting-based approaches and behavior cloning, as well as outperforming strong proprietary models. The paper also investigates the impact of behavior cloning, long chain-of-thought reasoning and explores a test-time scaling strategy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the combination of several techniques within a practical, end-to-end RL framework for web agents. While dynamic context compression and asynchronous rollouts aren't entirely new concepts in RL, their application to the specific problem of multi-turn web interaction is a worthwhile contribution. The extension of GRPO to the multi-turn setting (M-GRPO) is also a valuable contribution. A genuine novelty would require significant breakthrough in the underlying algorythm or a new perspective altogether. The exploration of long CoT and behavior cloning is another strength.
*   **Significance:** The results on WebArena-Lite are compelling and demonstrate a substantial improvement over existing baselines, including strong prompting and proprietary models. This demonstrates the effectiveness of the end-to-end RL approach for web agents, which are known for their complexity and the challenges of long-horizon decision-making. The analysis of training dynamics, the ablation studies (particularly those focusing on the importance of behavior cloning), and test-time scaling contribute valuable insights to the field. Also, the framework's simplicity makes it more practical and accessible.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The performance gains on WebArena-Lite are significant.
    *   **Practical framework:** The end-to-end approach, combined with techniques for efficient training, enhances practicality.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The ablation studies and analysis of training dynamics offer valuable insights.
    *   **Addresses a challenging problem:** Training web agents for complex, multi-turn interactions is a difficult but important area.
    *   **Minimalist approach:** Simplicity can be a strength, making the approach more accessible and easier to extend.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The individual components (dynamic context compression, asynchronous rollouts, M-GRPO) are more of evolutionary adaptations of existing techniques, so novelty is only in the combination and the context of web agent training.
    *   **Limited generalizability:** The results are demonstrated on WebArena-Lite, which is a specific benchmark. It would be valuable to see how well the framework generalizes to other web environments or tasks.
    *   **Reward design:** Binary rewards are a simplification. Exploring more sophisticated reward shaping could potentially further improve performance, although the paper explicitly mentions this as a potential direction for future work.
