- **Score**: 8/10

### R1-ShareVL: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability of Multimodal Large Language Models via Share-GRPO
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16673v1)
- **Authors**: Huanjin Yao, Qixiang Yin, Jingyi Zhang, Min Yang, Yibo Wang, Wenhao Wu, Fei Su, Li Shen, Minghui Qiu, Dacheng Tao, Jiaxing Huang
- **Abstract**: In this work, we aim to incentivize the reasoning ability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) via reinforcement learning (RL) and develop an effective approach that mitigates the sparse reward and advantage vanishing issues during RL. To this end, we propose Share-GRPO, a novel RL approach that tackle these issues by exploring and sharing diverse reasoning trajectories over expanded question space. Specifically, Share-GRPO first expands the question space for a given question via data transformation techniques, and then encourages MLLM to effectively explore diverse reasoning trajectories over the expanded question space and shares the discovered reasoning trajectories across the expanded questions during RL. In addition, Share-GRPO also shares reward information during advantage computation, which estimates solution advantages hierarchically across and within question variants, allowing more accurate estimation of relative advantages and improving the stability of policy training. Extensive evaluations over six widely-used reasoning benchmarks showcase the superior performance of our method. Code will be available at https://github.com/HJYao00/R1-ShareVL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the R1-ShareVL paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces R1-ShareVL, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  It aims to improve the reasoning capability of MLLMs by addressing the sparse reward and advantage vanishing problems that often arise during RL training. The core idea of R1-ShareVL is to expand the question space through semantically consistent transformations (both offline textual and online multimodal). This allows the MLLM to explore diverse reasoning trajectories and share the learned trajectories across the expanded question variants.  Furthermore, it introduces a hierarchical advantage estimation method, sharing reward information at both local (within question variant) and global (across question variants) levels. Experiments on several reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that R1-ShareVL outperforms existing RL-based approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's key novelty lies in the introduction of information sharing and question space expansion within the context of MLLM reinforcement learning.  While ideas like data augmentation and shared representation exist in other domains of deep learning, their application to the specific challenges of MLLM reasoning via RL is relatively new.  The hierarchical advantage estimation further refines the training process. The combined offline textual and online multimodal transformation appears novel.

* **Significance:**  The significance of the paper stems from the practical improvement in MLLM reasoning capabilities. Overcoming sparse rewards and advantage vanishing is a major hurdle in effectively training MLLMs with RL. By addressing these challenges, R1-ShareVL contributes to making RL a more viable method for improving MLLM reasoning, particularly for smaller models. The empirical results across multiple benchmarks support this claim.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problems of sparse rewards and advantage vanishing.
    * **Well-Motivated Approach:** The proposed Share-GRPO method is logically motivated by these problems.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental setup is extensive, covering multiple reasoning benchmarks and models of different sizes (7B and 32B).  Ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of different components of the approach.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The method consistently outperforms baseline models and other RL-based approaches.
    * **Code Availability:** Open-sourcing the code promotes reproducibility and further research.
    * **Dynamic Sampling Comparison:** A strong point is that the paper does not ignore the Dynamic Sampling approach and has clearly highlighted the strengths of their method over it, rather than simply stating that it performed better.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Rule-Based Rewards:**  The paper, like many others in this area, relies on rule-based reward functions. While these are easier to implement, they might not always capture the nuanced aspects of good reasoning.
    * **Transformation Dependence:** The performance of the method depends on the quality of the semantic transformations. If the generated question variants are poorly constructed, it could negatively impact the training process.
    * **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While the paper shows quantitative improvements, a deeper qualitative analysis of failure cases could further highlight the limitations of the approach.
    * **Justification for selected data transformation.** It would have been helpful to read a more thorough discussion of how and why the data transformations were selected and if any experimentation was conducted.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in MLLM training by encouraging the exploration of information sharing techniques and more sophisticated advantage estimation methods. The concept of question space expansion might also inspire novel data augmentation strategies tailored to MLLM reasoning.

**Overall:** The R1-ShareVL paper presents a solid contribution to the field of MLLM training. It effectively addresses the crucial challenges of sparse rewards and advantage vanishing, leading to significant improvements in reasoning performance. The comprehensive experiments and ablation studies strengthen the validity of the claims. While it is relying on existing approaches, the adaptations are innovative.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant method for improving MLLM reasoning using reinforcement learning. The approach is well-motivated and supported by strong empirical results. The weaknesses are primarily related to the dependence on rule-based rewards and the limitations of semantic transformations, which are not unique to this paper.  While not a groundbreaking theoretical advancement, the practical improvements and the clear presentation of the method make it a valuable contribution. The score is slightly above average, reflecting the solid contribution balanced against the reliance on existing components and the incremental advances in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Your Pre-trained LLM is Secretly an Unsupervised Confidence Calibrator
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16690v1)
- **Authors**: Beier Luo, Shuoyuan Wang, Yixuan Li, Hongxin Wei
- **Abstract**: Post-training of large language models is essential for adapting pre-trained language models (PLMs) to align with human preferences and downstream tasks. While PLMs typically exhibit well-calibrated confidence, post-trained language models (PoLMs) often suffer from over-confidence, assigning high confidence to both correct and incorrect outputs, which can undermine reliability in critical applications. A major obstacle in calibrating PoLMs is the scarcity of labeled data for individual downstream tasks. To address this, we propose Disagreement-Aware Confidence Alignment (DACA), a novel unsupervised method to optimize the parameters (e.g., temperature $\tau$) in post-hoc confidence calibration. Our method is motivated by the under-confidence issue caused by prediction disagreement between the PLM and PoLM while aligning their confidence via temperature scaling. Theoretically, the PLM's confidence underestimates PoLM's prediction accuracy on disagreement examples, causing a larger $\tau$ and producing under-confident predictions. DACA mitigates this by selectively using only agreement examples for calibration, effectively decoupling the influence of disagreement. In this manner, our method avoids an overly large $\tau$ in temperature scaling caused by disagreement examples, improving calibration performance. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our method, improving the average ECE of open-sourced and API-based LLMs (e.g. GPT-4o) by up to 15.08$\%$ on common benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the problem of confidence calibration in post-trained large language models (PoLMs). PoLMs, while excelling in downstream tasks, often suffer from overconfidence compared to their pre-trained counterparts (PLMs). The authors propose Disagreement-Aware Confidence Alignment (DACA), a novel unsupervised method that leverages the well-calibrated confidence of PLMs to calibrate PoLMs *without* requiring labeled data for the target task. DACA aligns the confidence of PoLMs with PLMs, focusing solely on examples where both models agree. The method is based on the theoretical finding that prediction disagreement between PLMs and PoLMs can negatively impact confidence alignment, leading to underconfidence if not handled carefully. Experiments show DACA's effectiveness in improving calibration across various open-source and API-based LLMs, even outperforming labeled temperature scaling in some cases and allowing efficient calibration of large-scale PoLMs where pre-trained and post-trained architectures differ.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using pre-trained LLM (PLM) confidence scores as a signal for calibrating post-trained LLMs (PoLM) is quite novel, especially the "disagreement-aware" aspect.  Leveraging agreement is a clever way to filter noisy signals from unlabeled data and is a significant departure from traditional calibration techniques that rely heavily on labeled data or auxiliary models.  The theoretical justification for *why* disagreement matters strengthens the contribution, going beyond a purely empirical result. The formulation of the unlabeled calibration problem is valuable.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very practical and important problem.  LLMs are increasingly deployed in sensitive applications, and trustworthy uncertainty estimates are crucial.  The reliance on labeled data for calibration presents a bottleneck, especially for specialized domains. A method that can effectively calibrate using readily available unlabeled data has significant real-world implications.  The fact that it works on both open-source and API-based models (GPT-4o) increases its practical value. The result showing improved selective classification as a consequence of better calibration is also significant, demonstrating a tangible benefit.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis to justify the DACA method.  This helps explain why naive confidence alignment can fail and provides a solid rationale for the proposed solution.
    *   **Practical and Flexible:**  DACA is applicable to both open-source and API-based models and can be integrated with other post-hoc calibration methods, increasing its flexibility. Its reliance on unlabeled data also makes it cost-effective.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on various LLMs, datasets, and metrics, supporting the effectiveness of DACA. They compare against relevant baselines. The analysis of performance across model sizes is also beneficial.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, despite the technical details.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Overhead:** The method relies on generating predictions from *both* the pre-trained and post-trained models, adding to the inference cost.  While computationally efficient compared to training-based calibration, this additional cost should be emphasized more.
    *   **Assumptions About PLM Calibration:** The method relies on the assumption that the PLM is well-calibrated. The paper acknowledges that the well-calibrated nature of PLMs is an inherent property.  However, there could be cases where this assumption is violated.  It would be good to have a discussion on the sensitivity of DACA to the calibration of the PLM or the types of tasks where PLM calibration might be unreliable.
    *   **Disagreement Examples:** The paper shows that filtering out disagreement examples is beneficial, but does not thoroughly investigate how these examples can be used to further improve performance. Some analysis in this direction would be helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** DACA has the potential to become a widely used technique for calibrating LLMs, especially in scenarios where labeled data is scarce. It could also spur further research into leveraging unlabeled data for confidence estimation and uncertainty quantification in LLMs. The theoretical insights could influence the design of other calibration methods.

*   **Justification of Score:** I'm giving a score of 8. The paper addresses a significant problem with a novel and well-justified method. The comprehensive evaluation and practical applicability of the method are clear strengths.  While the increased inference cost (from having to run both models) and reliance on the assumption of the PLM's calibration are limitations, these do not outweigh the overall contribution. This technique could significantly change how LLMs are calibrated in the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Beyond Induction Heads: In-Context Meta Learning Induces Multi-Phase Circuit Emergence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16694v1)
- **Authors**: Gouki Minegishi, Hiroki Furuta, Shohei Taniguchi, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based language models exhibit In-Context Learning (ICL), where predictions are made adaptively based on context. While prior work links induction heads to ICL through a sudden jump in accuracy, this can only account for ICL when the answer is included within the context. However, an important property of practical ICL in large language models is the ability to meta-learn how to solve tasks from context, rather than just copying answers from context; how such an ability is obtained during training is largely unexplored. In this paper, we experimentally clarify how such meta-learning ability is acquired by analyzing the dynamics of the model's circuit during training. Specifically, we extend the copy task from previous research into an In-Context Meta Learning setting, where models must infer a task from examples to answer queries. Interestingly, in this setting, we find that there are multiple phases in the process of acquiring such abilities, and that a unique circuit emerges in each phase, contrasting with the single-phases change in induction heads. The emergence of such circuits can be related to several phenomena known in large language models, and our analysis lead to a deeper understanding of the source of the transformer's ICL ability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how transformers acquire meta-learning capabilities in In-Context Learning (ICL), moving beyond simple copy tasks. The authors introduce an In-Context Meta-Learning (ICML) setting, where the model must infer the task from examples to answer queries. They train a simplified transformer in this setting and analyze the changes in its internal circuits during training. The key finding is that the model undergoes multiple learning phases, with distinct circuits emerging in each phase. These circuits include a bigram-focused circuit (ignoring context), a label-attention circuit (focusing on labels in the context), and a full-context circuit (chunking example pairs and using them for prediction). The authors introduce novel metrics to quantify these circuits and show that changes in these metrics align with sudden jumps in accuracy. They also investigate multi-head models, finding that different heads specialize in different circuits.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by exploring the circuit dynamics of transformers in a meta-learning ICL setting.  Prior work has focused primarily on induction heads and simple copy tasks.  The observation of multiple distinct circuits emerging during training, each corresponding to a different phase of learning, is a novel and important finding.  The introduction of new metrics to measure the emergence of these circuits is also valuable.

* **Significance:** The paper sheds light on the internal mechanisms that enable transformers to perform meta-learning in context. Understanding these mechanisms is crucial for improving the efficiency and robustness of ICL in large language models. The findings suggest that LLMs may initially learn to identify tasks by examining only labels, which aligns with previous observations that LLMs can perform well even with random label assignments. Furthermore, the observation of parallel specialization in multi-head models provides valuable insight into how LLMs can achieve smoother performance improvements.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of understanding how transformers acquire meta-learning capabilities in ICL and proposes a well-defined ICML setting to study this problem.
    * **Rigorous Experimental Design:** The authors conduct controlled experiments with a simplified transformer architecture and introduce novel metrics to quantify the emergence of different circuits.
    * **Novel Findings:** The paper presents novel findings regarding the multi-phase learning process and the emergence of distinct circuits in each phase. The observation of parallel specialization in multi-head models is also significant.
    * **Connections to LLM Behavior:** The paper connects its findings to several phenomena observed in LLMs, such as random-label robustness and multi-head attention, suggesting that the insights gained from the simplified model may generalize to larger models.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Simplified Model:** The paper uses a simplified transformer architecture, which may not fully capture the complexity of LLMs. The extent to which the findings generalize to larger and more complex models needs further investigation.
    * **Toy Task:** Although the introduced ICML setting is valuable, it remains a toy task and may not fully capture the nuances of real-world meta-learning problems.
    * **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses primarily on the circuit dynamics of transformers, without exploring other aspects of ICL, such as the role of attention patterns or the impact of different training datasets.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on ICL and mechanistic interpretability. The findings may lead to new methods for improving the efficiency and robustness of ICL in LLMs and for better understanding the internal mechanisms that enable LLMs to perform meta-learning. The introduced new metrics can prove useful in future studies.

* **Justification for Score:**  While the simplified model is a limitation, it's a *necessary* simplification to enable the type of circuit-level analysis the authors perform. The results are compelling and well-supported by the data. Most importantly, the paper pushes the boundary *beyond* induction heads, offering a much-needed new perspective on how task inference occurs in ICL. The connections to larger LLM phenomena, while not definitively proven, are strongly suggestive and add to the paper's significance. The core novel finding regarding distinct phases and metrics provides a solid contribution to the field. Therefore the findings are novel and provide a good basis for future work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Software Architecture Meets LLMs: A Systematic Literature Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16697v1)
- **Authors**: Larissa Schmid, Tobias Hey, Martin Armbruster, Sophie Corallo, Dominik Fuchß, Jan Keim, Haoyu Liu, Anne Koziolek
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are used for many different software engineering tasks. In software architecture, they have been applied to tasks such as classification of design decisions, detection of design patterns, and generation of software architecture design from requirements. However, there is little overview on how well they work, what challenges exist, and what open problems remain. In this paper, we present a systematic literature review on the use of LLMs in software architecture. We analyze 18 research articles to answer five research questions, such as which software architecture tasks LLMs are used for, how much automation they provide, which models and techniques are used, and how these approaches are evaluated. Our findings show that while LLMs are increasingly applied to a variety of software architecture tasks and often outperform baselines, some areas, such as generating source code from architectural design, cloud-native computing and architecture, and checking conformance remain underexplored. Although current approaches mostly use simple prompting techniques, we identify a growing research interest in refining LLM-based approaches by integrating advanced techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper presents a systematic literature review (SLR) of the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in software architecture (SA).  It analyzes 18 research articles to answer questions regarding: (1) the software architecture tasks LLMs are applied to; (2) the specific LLMs used; (3) techniques for optimizing LLM performance; (4) evaluation methodologies for LLM-based approaches; and (5) future research directions. The review finds that LLMs are applied across a range of SA tasks including reference architecture generation, classification & detection of architectural elements, extraction and generation of designs, and as assistive tools. The analysis reveals a trend towards decoder-only LLMs (especially GPT variants), with prompting being the dominant usage technique (particularly zero-shot). Evaluation commonly involves technical experiments and benchmarking. Finally, the paper discusses future research directions and implications of software architecture on LLM development.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Strengths:*

*   **Timeliness and Relevance:** The topic is highly relevant given the rapid adoption of LLMs in various aspects of software engineering. A dedicated review focusing on the intersection of LLMs and software architecture is timely and fills a gap in existing literature.
*   **Systematic Methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined and accepted methodology for conducting a systematic literature review (Kitchenham et al.), enhancing the credibility and rigor of the findings. The search strategy and filtering criteria are clearly articulated.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The review addresses several key research questions, providing a broad overview of the current state-of-the-art. The extracted data and presented figures offer a structured and informative summary of the analyzed literature.
*   **Discussion of Future Work:** The identification of future research directions provides valuable guidance for researchers looking to contribute to this evolving field.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope:** The number of papers reviewed (18) is relatively small for a systematic literature review. While the authors acknowledge the emerging nature of the field, the limited scope may affect the generalizability of the findings. The authors only reviewed articles from a selected set of venues, potentially excluding relevant studies.
*   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While the paper covers several aspects of LLM usage in SA, the analysis sometimes lacks depth. For instance, the section on optimization techniques (RQ3) could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the effectiveness of different prompting strategies and fine-tuning methods.
*   **Subjectivity in Classification:** Classifying papers into categories like "Classification & Detection," "Extraction & Generation," and "Assistants" inherently involves a degree of subjectivity. The authors could have further strengthened the analysis by discussing potential overlaps or ambiguities in the classification process.
*   **Lack of Critical Assessment of LLM Performance:**  While the review mentions evaluation metrics, there is limited critical assessment of *how well* LLMs are actually performing on these SA tasks. Are the performance improvements substantial, or only marginal? Are LLMs truly providing value beyond existing techniques, or are they simply adding complexity?  This crucial aspect is only superficially discussed.
*   **Limited Discussion of Threats:** There is only a superficial discussion of threats to validity. The authors could expand the discussion of limitations around the search strategy and the inherent subjectivity in selecting articles.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper's novelty lies in being the first systematic literature review specifically focusing on the intersection of LLMs and software architecture. While other SLRs cover LLMs in broader software engineering contexts, this review provides a targeted analysis of the challenges and opportunities in applying LLMs to SA tasks. The significance of the paper lies in providing a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners interested in leveraging LLMs for SA. The identification of research gaps and future directions can help guide future research efforts in the field.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence the field by: (1) Raising awareness of the applications of LLMs in SA; (2) Providing a structured overview of the current state-of-the-art; (3) Identifying key research challenges and opportunities; and (4) Encouraging more rigorous evaluation and benchmarking of LLM-based SA approaches.

*Score: 6*

**Rationale:**

The paper provides a valuable and timely contribution by conducting a much-needed systematic literature review. However, the relatively small number of analyzed papers and limited depth of critical analysis constrain the overall impact and value. There is no evaluation of any reference architectures for LLM systems. The lack of any critical analysis of performance improvements and any significant discussion about threats to validity further reduces its contribution to the field. Future work could enhance the analysis by expanding the scope of the review, conducting a more in-depth assessment of the strengths and limitations of different LLM techniques, and addressing challenges related to reproducibility and evaluation.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### MCP-RADAR: A Multi-Dimensional Benchmark for Evaluating Tool Use Capabilities in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16700v1)
- **Authors**: Xuanqi Gao, Siyi Xie, Juan Zhai, Shqing Ma, Chao Shen
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve from passive text generators to active reasoning agents capable of tool interaction, the Model Context Protocol (MCP) has emerged as a standardized framework for dynamic tool discovery and orchestration. Despite widespread industry adoption, existing evaluation methodologies fail to adequately assess tool utilization capabilities within this new paradigm. This paper introduces MCP-RADAR, the first comprehensive benchmark specifically designed to evaluate LLM performance in the MCP framework through a novel five-dimensional approach measuring: answer accuracy, tool selection efficiency, computational resource efficiency, parameter construction accuracy, and execution speed. Unlike conventional benchmarks that rely on subjective human evaluations or binary success metrics, MCP-RADAR employs objective, quantifiable measurements across multiple task domains including software engineering, mathematical reasoning, and general problem-solving. Our evaluations of leading commercial and open-source LLMs reveal distinctive capability profiles with significant trade-offs between accuracy, efficiency, and speed, challenging traditional single-metric performance rankings. Besides, we provide valuable guidance for developers to optimize their tools for maximum model compatibility and effectiveness. While focused on MCP due to its standardized approach, our methodology remains applicable across all LLM agent tool integration frameworks, providing valuable insights for both LLM developers and tool creators to optimize the entire LLM-tool interaction ecosystem. The implementation, configurations, and datasets used in our evaluation are publicly available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MCPRadar-B143.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MCP-RADAR: A Multi-Dimensional Benchmark for Evaluating Tool Use Capabilities in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MCP-RADAR, a novel benchmark for evaluating the tool-use capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) within the Model Context Protocol (MCP) framework. Unlike existing benchmarks that rely on subjective human evaluations or simple binary success metrics, MCP-RADAR employs a five-dimensional evaluation approach. These dimensions are: answer accuracy, tool selection efficiency, computational resource efficiency, parameter construction accuracy, and execution speed. The benchmark encompasses diverse tasks across software engineering, mathematical reasoning, and general problem-solving. The authors evaluated several leading commercial and open-source LLMs, revealing significant trade-offs between different performance metrics.  The paper also identifies connections between LLM capabilities and effective tool design. The code and dataset are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main strength is its focus on a largely unexplored area: standardized, objective evaluation of tool use within the emerging MCP framework. Existing LLM benchmarks primarily focus on knowledge-based reasoning or instruction following. MCP-RADAR directly addresses the gap in evaluating tool-augmented LLMs with a well-defined protocol. The multi-dimensional evaluation approach itself is a significant contribution, moving beyond simple success/failure metrics to analyze various aspects of tool utilization. The framework appears flexible, and new domains, tasks, and tools can be easily integrated.
*   **Significance:** The work has the potential to significantly impact both LLM developers and tool creators. For LLM developers, MCP-RADAR offers detailed insights into the strengths and weaknesses of models in areas like tool selection and parameter handling, guiding future model improvements. For tool creators, the benchmark provides valuable feedback on how tool design impacts model performance, promoting the development of more model-friendly tools and documentation. By providing objective metrics and a standardized evaluation framework, MCP-RADAR facilitates collaborative optimization within the LLM-tool interaction ecosystem. The publicly available code and dataset enable further research and development in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defined and objective evaluation metrics.
    *   Comprehensive benchmark covering multiple domains.
    *   Insightful analysis of model performance and trade-offs.
    *   Practical guidance for both LLM developers and tool creators.
    *   Publicly available resources for reproducibility and further research.
    *   Focuses on an important and rapidly evolving area of LLM development.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While focusing on objective metrics, the choice of what constitutes a "successful" task completion or "efficient" tool selection could still be debated or refined. Although the paper mentions scoring criteria, more details would improve clarity.
    *   The choice of the 7 LLMs evaluated could be seen as limited, especially if newer or more specialized models emerge quickly. The rapid pace of development in the LLM field makes it challenging to maintain a truly comprehensive benchmark.
    *   The paper acknowledges that the models are evaluated through a third-party service which introduces a possible point of failure in reproducing the work.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   Standardize the way tool-augmented LLMs are evaluated within the MCP paradigm.
    *   Drive improvements in both LLM architectures and tool design.
    *   Foster greater collaboration between LLM developers and tool creators.
    *   Encourage further research into multi-dimensional evaluation methodologies for LLMs.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** MCP-RADAR is a valuable and timely contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. Its focus on tool use, objective metrics, and multi-dimensional analysis represents a significant step forward. While there are some minor limitations (such as the potential subjectivity in defining success criteria and the rapid evolution of LLMs), the paper's strengths far outweigh its weaknesses. The potential impact on both LLM development and tool creation is substantial, making this a highly relevant and impactful work. The availability of the dataset and code will only amplify its influence within the research community.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Locate-then-Merge: Neuron-Level Parameter Fusion for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting in Multimodal LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16703v1)
- **Authors**: Zeping Yu, Sophia Ananiadou
- **Abstract**: Although multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved impressive performance, the multimodal instruction tuning stage often causes catastrophic forgetting of the base LLM's language ability, even in strong models like Llama3. To address this, we propose Locate-then-Merge, a training-free parameter fusion framework that first locates important parameters and then selectively merges them. We further introduce Neuron-Fusion, a neuron-level strategy that preserves the influence of neurons with large parameter shifts--neurons likely responsible for newly acquired visual capabilities--while attenuating the influence of neurons with smaller changes that likely encode general-purpose language skills. This design enables better retention of visual adaptation while mitigating language degradation. Experiments on 13 benchmarks across both language and visual tasks show that Neuron-Fusion consistently outperforms existing model merging methods. Further analysis reveals that our method effectively reduces context hallucination in generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Locate-then-Merge: Neuron-Level Parameter Fusion for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting in Multimodal LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of catastrophic forgetting in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) that occurs during multimodal instruction tuning. The authors propose a training-free parameter fusion framework called "Locate-then-Merge" which decouples the process into identifying important parameters and selectively merging them. Specifically, they introduce "Neuron-Fusion," a neuron-level strategy that preserves neurons with large parameter shifts (likely responsible for new visual capabilities) while suppressing those with smaller changes (likely encoding general language skills). The method is evaluated on 13 benchmarks across language and vision tasks using Llama3 and Mistral 7B. The results demonstrate that Neuron-Fusion outperforms existing model merging techniques, mitigating language degradation and reducing context hallucination.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of identifying and selectively merging parameters based on neuron-level changes is reasonably novel. While model merging and catastrophic forgetting are well-studied areas, the *specific* approach of focusing on parameter deltas *at the neuron level* and hypothesizing that large changes are more crucial for retaining new capabilities while small changes cause forgetting, is a valuable addition.  The decomposition into Locate-then-Merge framework is a good abstraction for understanding many merging methods. However, the idea of selectively averaging parameters isn't entirely new, so the novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary.

* **Significance:** Catastrophic forgetting is a significant bottleneck in the development and deployment of MLLMs. Addressing it is crucial for building models that can effectively leverage multimodal information without sacrificing general language abilities. The paper's demonstration that Neuron-Fusion can mitigate this issue is valuable and the results on Llama3 and Mistral 7B give reasonable confidence of practical applicability. The finding about Neuron-Fusion mitigating two critical issues in multimodal generation: uncertainty in answering and hallucination beyond the provided context, has significant practical implications.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of catastrophic forgetting in MLLMs.
    * **Well-Motivated Approach:** The Neuron-Fusion strategy is based on a plausible hypothesis about how different neurons contribute to visual adaptation and language ability.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are comprehensive, using a diverse set of benchmarks and two popular LLMs.  The ablation studies provide further insights into the method's behavior. Quantitative results show the improvement over the existing model merging techniques.
    * **Insightful Analysis:** The generation analysis sheds light on the method's ability to reduce context hallucination, which is a significant strength.
    * **Training-Free:** Being a training-free method enables quick and efficient model integration in scenarios with limited computational resources.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generality:** The experiments are focused on vision and the visual instruction tuning paradigm. While the authors mention that the approach could be extended to other modalities, this is not demonstrated in the paper.
    * **Limited Architectures:** The method is evaluated on decoder-only LLMs.  Applicability to encoder-decoder models or other architectures isn't explored.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The method introduces several hyperparameters (K and M), and while the paper includes ablation studies, the optimal values may vary across different datasets and models.
    * **Incremental Novelty:** The core idea builds upon existing model merging techniques. While the neuron-level approach is innovative, it's not a fundamentally new paradigm.
    * **Practical Applicability:**  Model merging in general, and Neuron-Fusion in particular, adds complexity to the model deployment pipeline. The practical benefits in terms of performance gains need to be weighed against this added complexity.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research on MLLMs by:
    * Encouraging the development of neuron-level techniques for model merging and continual learning.
    * Providing a practical method for mitigating catastrophic forgetting in MLLMs.
    * Highlighting the importance of reducing context hallucination in multimodal generation.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper. While the paper is well-written, thoroughly evaluated and addresses a significant problem in MLLMs, it is not groundbreaking in terms of conceptual novelty. The main contribution lies in the neuron-level parameter selection and the Locate-then-Merge framework which, though effective, are evolutionary rather than revolutionary in the landscape of model merging. The limitations regarding modality generality and architectural specificity also somewhat temper the score. However, the strong empirical results, the practical nature of the method, and the insightful analysis of context hallucination make it a significant contribution to the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Training Long-Context LLMs Efficiently via Chunk-wise Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16710v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhao Li, Yuxin Zhang, Gen Luo, Daohai Yu, Rongrong Ji
- **Abstract**: While long-context large language models (LLMs) exhibit remarkable document processing capabilities, their prohibitively high training costs often hinder customized applications. To mitigate this issue, we propose \textit{Sequential Chunk-wise Optimization} (SeCO), a memory-efficient training paradigm that partitions lengthy inputs into manageable chunks. Each chunk independently constructs its computational graph and performs localized backpropagation, ensuring that only one chunk's forward activations are stored in memory. Building on SeCO, we further introduce \textit{Sparse Chunk-wise Optimization} (SpaCO), which reduces computational overhead by selectively propagating gradients to specific chunks and incorporates a carefully designed compensation factor to ensure unbiased gradient estimation. SpaCO decouples the computational cost of backpropagation from the context length, enabling training time to gradually converge to inference time as sequences become longer. Implemented as lightweight training wrappers, both SeCO and SpaCO offer substantial practical benefits. For example, when fine-tuning an 8B model with LoRA on a single RTX 3090 GPU, SeCO expands maximum sequence length from 1K to 16K tokens, while SpaCO demonstrates accelerated training speed -- achieving up to 3x faster than SeCO under the same experimental setup. These innovations provide new insights into optimizing long-context models, making them more accessible for practical applications. We have open-sourced the code at \href{https://github.com/wenhaoli-xmu/seco}{here}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces two novel training paradigms, Sequential Chunk-wise Optimization (SeCO) and Sparse Chunk-wise Optimization (SpaCO), to efficiently train long-context Large Language Models (LLMs). SeCO partitions long input sequences into smaller, manageable chunks and performs localized backpropagation to reduce memory consumption. SpaCO builds on SeCO by selectively propagating gradients to specific chunks and incorporates a compensation factor to ensure unbiased gradient estimation, thus reducing computational overhead. The authors demonstrate that both methods significantly reduce memory usage and can improve training speed compared to existing techniques. They enable the fine-tuning of larger models on longer sequences with limited resources (e.g., a single RTX 3090 GPU).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The core idea of chunk-wise optimization, particularly combined with gradient checkpointing along the sequence dimension, is reasonably novel.  While gradient checkpointing is a known technique, its application along the sequence dimension with overlapping KV caches and the specific strategies outlined in SeCO represents a non-trivial adaptation.  SpaCO adds another layer of novelty through the introduction of sparse backpropagation and the corresponding theoretical analysis and compensation factor. The analysis of bounded gradient chain length and its implications for unbiased estimation is a significant contribution.  The integration of these ideas into a practical training framework is also commendable.

*Significance:*

The paper addresses a major bottleneck in the LLM field: the high computational and memory costs associated with training long-context models.  The ability to fine-tune larger models on longer sequences with limited resources has substantial practical implications. The empirical results, demonstrating both memory savings and speedups, support the paper's claims and further emphasize its potential impact.  The open-sourced code significantly increases the accessibility and reproducibility of their methods, which is also a strong positive point. The theoretical analysis of SpaCO and its guarantees of unbiased gradient estimation add further weight. While the paper acknowledges that the methods still trade-off computation time, memory, and gradient accuracy, they provide a practical approach to balance these factors, which is significant for resource-constrained users.

*Weaknesses:*

1.  **Limited Comparative Scope:** While the paper compares to DeepSpeed and gradient checkpointing, comparisons to more recent and highly efficient long-context methods, such as attention approximation techniques or efficient hardware-aware implementations, are limited.
2.  **Performance Gap:** Though SpaCO theoretically ensures unbiased gradient estimation, in practice, it introduces a performance gap to exact gradient training. The paper mitigates these impacts through careful experiments and hyperparameter tuning.
3.  **Omitted Hardware Details:** The paper mentions the specific RTX 3090's configuration but misses a discussion on more general hardware optimizations.

*Overall Assessment:*

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by providing practical and theoretically sound methods for training long-context LLMs with limited resources. The combination of chunk-wise optimization, gradient checkpointing, and sparse backpropagation offers a compelling approach to address the memory and computational bottlenecks in this area.  Despite some limitations in comparative scope and the performance trade-off in SpaCO, the paper presents a valuable contribution with clear potential to impact future research and practical applications. The open-sourced code further strengthens its value.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Breaking mBad! Supervised Fine-tuning for Cross-Lingual Detoxification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16722v1)
- **Authors**: Himanshu Beniwal, Youngwoo Kim, Maarten Sap, Soham Dan, Thomas Hartvigsen
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly prevalent in global applications, ensuring that they are toxicity-free across diverse linguistic contexts remains a critical challenge. We explore "Cross-lingual Detoxification", a cross-lingual paradigm that mitigates toxicity, enabling detoxification capabilities to transfer between high and low-resource languages across different script families. We analyze cross-lingual detoxification's effectiveness through 504 extensive settings to evaluate toxicity reduction in cross-distribution settings with limited data and investigate how mitigation impacts model performance on non-toxic tasks, revealing trade-offs between safety and knowledge preservation. Our code and dataset are publicly available at https://github.com/himanshubeniwal/Breaking-mBad.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "Breaking mBad! Supervised Fine-tuning for Cross-Lingual Detoxification" investigates the effectiveness of cross-lingual detoxification (X-DET) for large language models (LLMs). The authors fine-tune LLMs on parallel toxic-neutral pairs in one source language and then evaluate the transfer of detoxification capabilities to nine target languages across various script families. They analyze the impact of linguistic properties, data scarcity, and different fine-tuning strategies (cross-lingual, multilingual, and percent-based) on toxicity reduction and knowledge preservation.  The authors demonstrate that cross-lingual detoxification significantly outperforms other methods, especially when using limited parallel data, and that script similarity influences transfer effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The exploration of cross-lingual detoxification is a valuable contribution, as most previous work has focused on monolingual toxicity mitigation.  The systematic investigation of the impact of script family similarity on transfer effectiveness is relatively novel. However, the core technique of supervised fine-tuning is well-established; the innovation lies primarily in the experimental setup and analysis.

*   **Significance:**  As LLMs are increasingly deployed globally, ensuring cross-lingual safety is crucial. The paper addresses this challenge directly, providing empirical evidence for the viability of X-DET. Identifying factors that influence transfer, like script similarity, offers practical insights for developing more effective cross-lingual detoxification strategies, especially for low-resource languages.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Extensive Experimental Setup:** The paper presents a comprehensive analysis across a variety of languages, models, and fine-tuning strategies, increasing confidence in the results. The 504 experimental configurations demonstrate a rigorous exploration of the topic.
    *   **Focus on Limited Data Scenarios:** The investigation of performance with only 10%, 20%, or 30% of the parallel data available is particularly important, as this reflects the realities of low-resource language settings.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper analyzes not only toxicity reduction but also the impact on perplexity, offering a balanced view of safety and knowledge preservation trade-offs. The representation analysis using embedding distances provides insights into how fine-tuning affects the model's understanding of toxicity across languages.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Techniques:** While supervised fine-tuning is a solid baseline, exploring more advanced detoxification techniques like reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) or contrastive learning could potentially yield even better results. The absence of DPO is notable.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Dependence:** The reliance on Perspective API for toxicity evaluation is a potential limitation, as this API might not accurately reflect nuances in toxicity across all languages and cultures. Ideally, human evaluation or the use of more diverse and language-specific toxicity detection tools would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Presentation Challenges:**  While comprehensive, the sheer volume of experimental results can be overwhelming and difficult to digest.  The paper would benefit from more concise summaries and visualizations highlighting the most important trends. The formatting and figure clarity can be improved.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** It is unclear why the results of some models (AYA-23-8B) shows that the average A-toxicity scores increases under the X-FT setting. Performing ablation studies to remove certain settings and examine their impact would allow readers to better understand what makes a model perform well with cross-lingual detoxification.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by highlighting the importance of cross-lingual detoxification and the impact of script family similarity. It also provides a valuable benchmark for comparing different detoxification approaches in multilingual settings. The code and dataset availability further enhance the impact by enabling others to build upon the work.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by addressing the important problem of cross-lingual toxicity in LLMs. The extensive experimental analysis and focus on limited data scenarios are significant strengths. However, the relative lack of technical novelty, the limitations of the evaluation metric, and some presentation challenges hold back the overall impact. The potential of the work is high, but the current implementation and analysis leave room for further exploration and improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Robust LLM Fingerprinting via Domain-Specific Watermarks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16723v1)
- **Authors**: Thibaud Gloaguen, Robin Staab, Nikola Jovanović, Martin Vechev
- **Abstract**: As open-source language models (OSMs) grow more capable and are widely shared and finetuned, ensuring model provenance, i.e., identifying the origin of a given model instance, has become an increasingly important issue. At the same time, existing backdoor-based model fingerprinting techniques often fall short of achieving key requirements of real-world model ownership detection. In this work, we build on the observation that while current open-source model watermarks fail to achieve reliable content traceability, they can be effectively adapted to address the challenge of model provenance. To this end, we introduce the concept of domain-specific watermarking for model fingerprinting. Rather than watermarking all generated content, we train the model to embed watermarks only within specified subdomains (e.g., particular languages or topics). This targeted approach ensures detection reliability, while improving watermark durability and quality under a range of real-world deployment settings. Our evaluations show that domain-specific watermarking enables model fingerprinting with strong statistical guarantees, controllable false positive rates, high detection power, and preserved generation quality. Moreover, we find that our fingerprints are inherently stealthy and naturally robust to real-world variability across deployment scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Robust LLM Fingerprinting via Domain-Specific Watermarks":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of model provenance in open-source language models (OSMs), a growing concern as these models become more capable and widely shared. Existing backdoor-based fingerprinting methods have limitations in real-world deployments. This paper proposes domain-specific watermarking, a novel approach where models are trained to embed watermarks only within specified subdomains (e.g., specific languages or topics). This targeted approach enhances detection reliability, durability, and generation quality while preserving stealthiness and robustness against real-world variations. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their method through evaluations showcasing strong statistical guarantees, controlled false positive rates, high detection power, and preserved generation quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of domain-specific watermarking is a significant advancement over existing LLM watermarking techniques for fingerprinting. By relaxing the requirement of watermarking *all* generated content, the authors address key limitations of current watermarks, including performance degradation and vulnerability to fine-tuning. The adaptation of generation-time watermarks, specifically KGW, for model provenance through domain restriction is a creative and non-trivial contribution.

*   **Significance:** The research is highly relevant and timely. Model provenance is critical for GenAI safety, intellectual property protection, and responsible AI deployment, given the increasing proliferation of open-source models and potential licensing violations. This work offers a practical black-box fingerprinting solution, overcoming limitations of prior techniques that require model access or introduce unnatural behaviors. Domain-specific watermarks could become a valuable tool for model providers to protect their investments and enforce licensing terms.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practicality:** The method targets a realistic black-box setting and provides a concrete instantiation of a domain-specific watermark.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation, covering reliability, persistence (robustness against fine-tuning), and stealthiness. They address issues such as system prompts and greedy sampling, which are often overlooked in the literature.
    *   **Statistical Guarantees:** Leveraging the statistical properties of generation-time watermarks, the method provides strong control over false positive rates.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Desiderata:** The paper clearly articulates the key requirements for practical LLM fingerprinting.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Domain Selection Reliance:** The effectiveness of the approach depends on selecting a suitable watermark domain that is both high-entropy and less relevant to typical use cases. This could limit its applicability in certain scenarios. The math finetuning being the least persistent highlights this concern.
    *   **Domain Overlap:** The paper acknowledges the limitation imposed by "domain leakage", where the regularization dataset contains data from the target domain. Although the author's provide a solution to this, it also brings a certain degree of limitation.
    *   **Generalization Claims:** While the paper presents results across multiple models and domains, further investigation could explore how domain-specific watermarks behave with models of different architectures and scales.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence future research and development in model fingerprinting and provenance. It establishes a new direction for adapting generation-time watermarks, emphasizing domain awareness and practical considerations. The insights and evaluation framework provided in the paper will likely serve as a foundation for future investigations in this area. The paper also shows how versatile watermarking can be through things such as a trigger token.

*   **Overall**: The combination of theoretical and practical considerations for the implementation and testing of domain specific watermarks is novel and impactful.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to LLM fingerprinting with a comprehensive evaluation demonstrating its effectiveness. While there are some limitations related to domain selection and the degradation in cases of domain overlap, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The contribution is a significant advancement in the field and is likely to influence future research and adoption of watermark-based model provenance techniques.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Masked Conditioning for Deep Generative Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16725v1)
- **Authors**: Phillip Mueller, Jannik Wiese, Sebastian Mueller, Lars Mikelsons
- **Abstract**: Datasets in engineering domains are often small, sparsely labeled, and contain numerical as well as categorical conditions. Additionally. computational resources are typically limited in practical applications which hinders the adoption of generative models for engineering tasks. We introduce a novel masked-conditioning approach, that enables generative models to work with sparse, mixed-type data. We mask conditions during training to simulate sparse conditions at inference time. For this purpose, we explore the use of various sparsity schedules that show different strengths and weaknesses. In addition, we introduce a flexible embedding that deals with categorical as well as numerical conditions. We integrate our method into an efficient variational autoencoder as well as a latent diffusion model and demonstrate the applicability of our approach on two engineering-related datasets of 2D point clouds and images. Finally, we show that small models trained on limited data can be coupled with large pretrained foundation models to improve generation quality while retaining the controllability induced by our conditioning scheme.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a "masked conditioning" approach for deep generative models (DGMs) to address the challenges of using DGMs in engineering design. Engineering datasets are often small, sparsely labeled, and contain mixed data types (numerical and categorical). The masked conditioning technique involves randomly masking conditions during training to simulate sparse conditions at inference time, making the model robust to incomplete data. The authors explore various sparsity schedules and introduce a flexible embedding to handle mixed data types. They integrate their method into variational autoencoders (VAEs) and latent diffusion models (LDMs) and demonstrate its applicability on engineering-related datasets (2D point clouds and images of bicycles and cars). Finally, they show that small models trained on limited data can be coupled with large pretrained foundation models to improve generation quality while retaining controllability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core idea of masked conditioning isn't entirely new in the broader context of machine learning. Masking techniques have been explored in various forms, especially in language models and some image completion tasks. *However*, its targeted application *specifically to the challenges faced in engineering design datasets* is where the potential novelty lies. Specifically, the design of sparsity schedules to account for variable data availability during inference and the flexible embedding scheme designed to handle a mix of numerical and categorical data are worthwhile contributions. It's an adaptation and refinement of existing concepts to a *specific problem domain.*

**Significance:**

The significance hinges on the practical impact it could have on engineering design. The paper correctly identifies a real hurdle: the lack of large, high-quality datasets in many engineering applications. If the proposed method effectively allows engineers to leverage DGMs with limited and imperfect data, it could open up new possibilities for design exploration, optimization, and automation. Furthermore, the approach of combining small, domain-specific models with large, pretrained foundation models is significant in addressing the computational limitations, allowing smaller firms to leverage pre-trained resources.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem-Focused:** The paper directly addresses a real and relevant problem in engineering design.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The masking conditioning approach is described clearly with relevant design choices discussed in the work.
*   **Good Implementation & Integration:** The method is integrated into both VAEs and LDMs, showcasing its versatility.
*   **Demonstrated Applicability:** The experiments on two engineering-related datasets (bicycle and car designs) provide evidence of its potential.
*   **Combining Small & Large Models:** The idea of using small, specialized models as priors for large, pretrained models is a promising approach for balancing performance and resource requirements.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Quantitative Results:** While qualitative results are presented, the quantitative results shown lack detailed analysis, especially for the diffusion model experiments. More analysis would be helpful, especially a comparison of models with and without masked conditioning.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** The core concept of masking is not groundbreaking. The novelty is mostly in its specific application and adaptations, but this limits the paper's overall impact.
*   **Lack of Extrapolation:** The authors admit that their models lack extrapolation capabilities and would perform poorly on completely novel conditions. This is a significant limitation for real-world engineering design scenarios where exploring new designs is critical.
*   **Dependency on Pretrained Models:** In LDM the performance is constrained by the pretrained VAE used for encoding. Finetuning is difficult, which means the final performance hinges on the base VAE model used.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While various sparsity schedules are examined, more thorough ablation studies are needed to understand the contribution of each component of the method.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, I'm assigning a **Score: 6**.

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed adaptation of existing masking techniques to a specific and important problem in engineering design. Its strengths lie in its practicality, clear methodology, and demonstrated applicability on relevant datasets. The method offers a tangible solution to the challenge of limited data in engineering, which is significant.

However, the incremental nature of the core novelty (masking itself isn't new), the limitations in quantitative evaluation (especially in the diffusion model experiments), the acknowledged lack of extrapolation capabilities, and the dependency on pre-trained components hold it back. The limited ablation studies and detailed statistical analysis further limit the score. A score of 6 reflects that this is a worthwhile and potentially useful contribution, but not a game-changing or fundamentally groundbreaking advancement. There is room for improvement in the evaluation of results, ablation studies and exploration of more sophisticated techniques to address the issues discussed.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Forward-only Diffusion Probabilistic Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16733v1)
- **Authors**: Ziwei Luo, Fredrik K. Gustafsson, Jens Sjölund, Thomas B. Schön
- **Abstract**: This work presents a forward-only diffusion (FoD) approach for generative modelling. In contrast to traditional diffusion models that rely on a coupled forward-backward diffusion scheme, FoD directly learns data generation through a single forward diffusion process, yielding a simple yet efficient generative framework. The core of FoD is a state-dependent linear stochastic differential equation that involves a mean-reverting term in both the drift and diffusion functions. This mean-reversion property guarantees the convergence to clean data, naturally simulating a stochastic interpolation between source and target distributions. More importantly, FoD is analytically tractable and is trained using a simple stochastic flow matching objective, enabling a few-step non-Markov chain sampling during inference. The proposed FoD model, despite its simplicity, achieves competitive performance on various image-conditioned (e.g., image restoration) and unconditional generation tasks, demonstrating its effectiveness in generative modelling. Our code is available at https://github.com/Algolzw/FoD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Forward-only Diffusion Probabilistic Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel generative modeling approach called Forward-Only Diffusion (FoD). Unlike traditional diffusion models that rely on coupled forward and backward processes, FoD directly learns data generation using a single, analytically tractable forward diffusion process. The core of FoD is a state-dependent linear Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE) incorporating a mean-reverting term in both the drift and diffusion functions. This mean-reversion guarantees convergence to clean data and simulates stochastic interpolation between distributions. The model is trained using a stochastic flow matching objective, enabling few-step sampling. The authors demonstrate competitive performance on image-conditioned and unconditional generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the "forward-only" formulation. The departure from the standard forward-backward diffusion paradigm is a significant simplification, particularly for image-conditioned generation. The use of a mean-reverting SDE, where the mean-reversion affects both drift and diffusion, is also a notable design choice. While flow matching exists, the stochastic extension with the SDE is a novel contribution.
*   **Significance:**  The potential significance is substantial. Simplifying the diffusion process leads to more efficient training and sampling. The few-step sampling capability is a major advantage.  The competitive performance on image restoration tasks, where traditional diffusion models can be cumbersome, highlights the practical value. The connection to flow matching also provides a useful theoretical link.

**Strengths:**

*   **Simplicity:** FoD offers a conceptually simpler approach to generative modeling.
*   **Efficiency:** The single forward process and few-step sampling capabilities lead to significant efficiency gains.
*   **Analytical Tractability:**  The analytically tractable nature of the model allows for a better understanding and control over the generative process.
*   **Image-Conditioned Performance:** The competitive performance on image restoration tasks demonstrates a practical advantage over traditional diffusion models.
*   **Clear Derivations:** The paper includes clear derivations, increasing transparency and reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **CIFAR-10 Performance:** The unconditional generation results on CIFAR-10 are not state-of-the-art. While the authors acknowledge this and attribute it to architectural choices and hyperparameter tuning, it is a weakness compared to established diffusion models. This raises questions about the generalizability of FoD to more complex datasets.
*   **Dependence on Stochastic Noise Injection:** The crucial role of noise injection, as demonstrated by the degraded performance of the ODE variant, might limit applications where noise is undesirable.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While analytically tractable, a deeper theoretical understanding of the properties of the mean-reverting SDE and its convergence behavior would be beneficial.
*   **Incremental vs. Revolutionary:** While novel, it builds upon the existing framework of flow matching and SDEs. Is it a revolutionary breakthrough, or a valuable incremental improvement?

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of generative modeling. It offers a compelling alternative to traditional diffusion models, achieving competitive performance with a simpler and more efficient framework, especially well-suited to image restoration. The analytical tractability is a significant strength, and the stochastic extension of flow matching is a novel idea.

However, it's not without its shortcomings. The weaker CIFAR-10 performance and reliance on noise injection raise questions about its generalizability and applicability in certain scenarios. The improvements appear to be valuable but incremental.

Therefore, considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 7 is justified. It's a solid, well-executed paper with clear contributions, but more substantial improvements are needed before it reaches groundbreaking status.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mitigating Fine-tuning Risks in LLMs via Safety-Aware Probing Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16737v1)
- **Authors**: Chengcan Wu, Zhixin Zhang, Zeming Wei, Yihao Zhang, Meng Sun
- **Abstract**: The significant progress of large language models (LLMs) has led to remarkable achievements across numerous applications. However, their ability to generate harmful content has sparked substantial safety concerns. Despite the implementation of safety alignment techniques during the pre-training phase, recent research indicates that fine-tuning LLMs on adversarial or even benign data can inadvertently compromise their safety. In this paper, we re-examine the fundamental issue of why fine-tuning on non-harmful data still results in safety degradation. We introduce a safety-aware probing (SAP) optimization framework designed to mitigate the safety risks of fine-tuning LLMs. Specifically, SAP incorporates a safety-aware probe into the gradient propagation process, mitigating the model's risk of safety degradation by identifying potential pitfalls in gradient directions, thereby enhancing task-specific performance while successfully preserving model safety. Our extensive experimental results demonstrate that SAP effectively reduces harmfulness below the original fine-tuned model and achieves comparable test loss to standard fine-tuning methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/ChengcanWu/SAP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the safety risks associated with fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs), even when using benign data. It identifies the entanglement of useful-critical and safety-critical gradient directions as a key reason for safety degradation during fine-tuning. The paper proposes a Safety-Aware Probing (SAP) optimization framework that integrates a safety-aware probe into the gradient propagation process. SAP aims to mitigate the risk of safety degradation by identifying problematic gradient directions, enhancing task-specific performance while preserving model safety. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SAP effectively reduces harmfulness while maintaining comparable performance to standard fine-tuning methods. The code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution lies in its analysis of the entanglement between "useful-critical" and "safety-critical" gradient directions and the introduction of SAP as a means to disentangle these effects. While the idea of using probes and adversarial training concepts isn't entirely new (e.g., related to sharpness-aware minimization and adversarial training), applying them *specifically* to address the safety degradation problem during LLM fine-tuning and the proposed mechanism with safety-aware probe introduces significant innovation. The approach of using a probe to influence the gradient direction is well-motivated, and the connection to weight perturbations in SAM is insightful. The ablation studies provided are thorough enough to give confidence in the design.

*   **Significance:** The problem of safety degradation in fine-tuned LLMs is a significant concern, hindering their widespread deployment. The paper presents a practical approach to mitigate these risks without sacrificing task-specific performance. The experimental results showing reduced harmfulness and comparable performance to standard fine-tuning are compelling. The robustness against adversarial attacks further increases the value. The fact that SAP appears compatible with other existing safety methods and LoRA adaptation increases its significance. The open-source availability of the code enhances the potential for adoption and further research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed and theoretically grounded approach.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and models.
    *   Demonstrated robustness against adversarial attacks.
    *   Good ablation studies and analysis of the approach.
    *   Publicly available code.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The computational cost of SAP is higher than SFT, which could limit its applicability in some resource-constrained scenarios. However, that is generally an acceptable tradeoff for improved safety.
    *   While the paper demonstrates effectiveness, a deeper theoretical understanding of *why* and *when* this entanglement of gradients occurs would further strengthen the work. Some initial thoughts are already provided, though.
    *   The experiments, while comprehensive, could benefit from further analysis of the specific types of harmful content that SAP is most effective at mitigating.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to impact the development and deployment of safer LLMs by providing a practical and effective technique for mitigating safety risks during fine-tuning. The method's compatibility with existing safety methods and LoRA increases its relevance to real-world applications.

**Justification for the score:**

SAP presents a novel, well-motivated, and empirically supported approach to address a critical problem in LLM safety. While the method builds upon existing concepts like weight perturbations, its application in the context of fine-tuning and the mechanism of safety-aware probes constitutes a significant advancement. It's computationally more expensive than fine-tuning, but the demonstrable improvements in safety justify the added cost. Overall, SAP represents a valuable and practical contribution to the field.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### TRIM: Achieving Extreme Sparsity with Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven Pruning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16743v1)
- **Authors**: Florentin Beck, William Rudman, Carsten Eickhoff
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) present significant computational and memory challenges due to their extensive size, making pruning essential for their efficient deployment. Existing one-shot pruning methods often apply uniform sparsity constraints across layers or within each layer, resulting in suboptimal performance, especially at high sparsity ratios. This work introduces TRIM (Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven pruning), a novel approach that applies varying sparsity ratios to individual output dimensions (rows) within each layer. TRIM employs an iterative adjustment process guided by quality metrics to optimize dimension-wise sparsity allocation, focusing on reducing variance in quality retention across outputs to preserve critical information. TRIM can be seamlessly integrated with existing layer-wise pruning strategies. Our evaluations on perplexity and zero-shot tasks across diverse LLM families (Qwen2.5, LLaMA-2, and OPT) and sparsity levels demonstrate that TRIM achieves new state-of-the-art results and enhances stability. For instance, at 80% sparsity, TRIM reduces perplexity by 48% for Qwen2.5-14B and over 90% for OPT-13B compared to baseline methods. We conclude that fine-grained, dimension-wise sparsity adaptation is crucial for pushing the limits of extreme LLM compression. Code available at: https://github.com/flobk/TRIM
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRIM (Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven Pruning), a novel approach to pruning large language models (LLMs).  TRIM aims to achieve extreme sparsity by applying varying sparsity ratios to individual output dimensions (rows) within each layer, unlike existing methods that typically apply uniform sparsity constraints. TRIM employs an iterative adjustment process guided by quality metrics to optimize dimension-wise sparsity allocation, focusing on reducing variance in quality retention across outputs. The paper demonstrates that TRIM can be seamlessly integrated with existing layer-wise pruning strategies and achieves state-of-the-art results on perplexity and zero-shot tasks across diverse LLM families and sparsity levels. The authors also provide an analysis of why TRIM works better, attributing it to the varying sensitivity of output dimensions to pruning and their differing importance in downstream tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in the concept of dimension-wise (row-wise) sparsity adaptation in one-shot LLM pruning.
